  2. $\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A) = n$ always
  3. $\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A) \le \max\{m, n\}$ only
  4. $\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A) = m + n$
Explanation. Row rank and column rank coincide.
la-f-matrices-k32 · level ipotesi · notes
For $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n \times n}$, which condition is equivalent to $\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A) = n$?
  1. $\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$
  2. $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$
  3. $\boldsymbol A$ symmetric
  4. $\boldsymbol A$ with positive entries
Explanation. Independent columns if and only if the matrix is invertible.
la-f-matrices-k33 · level ipotesi · notes
To conclude that $\boldsymbol Q = \boldsymbol A'\boldsymbol A$ is positive semidefinite for every $\boldsymbol A$, what is used?
  1. $\boldsymbol x'\boldsymbol A'\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \|\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x\|_2^2 \ge 0$
  2. That $\boldsymbol A$ is square
  3. That $\boldsymbol A$ is invertible
  4. That $\boldsymbol A$ has positive entries
Explanation. A sum of squares is nonnegative.
la-f-matrices-k34 · level facile · notes
For $\boldsymbol A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, which matrix $\boldsymbol B$ satisfies $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B = \boldsymbol B\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol I$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} \frac12 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 & -1 \\ -1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & -1 \\ -1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. $\det\boldsymbol A = 1$: swap the diagonal entries and change the sign of the others.
la-f-matrices-k35 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that a square matrix with a zero column is not invertible, which fact suffices?
  1. Its trace is $0$
  2. Its determinant is $0$
  3. It is not symmetric
  4. It has negative entries
Explanation. Expanding along the zero column gives $\det = 0$.
la-f-matrices-k36 · level facile · notes
Row $i$ of a matrix $\boldsymbol A$ is denoted by $R_i(\boldsymbol A)$. For $\boldsymbol A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 0 & -1 \\ 5 & 3 & 4 \end{pmatrix}$, what is $R_2(\boldsymbol A)$?
  1. $(0,\, 3)'$
  2. $(2 \;\; 0 \;\; -1)$
  3. $(-1,\, 4)'$
  4. $(5 \;\; 3 \;\; 4)$
Explanation. The second row is $(5 \;\, 3 \;\, 4)$. $(0, 3)'$ is the second column $C_2(\boldsymbol A)$; $(2 \;\, 0 \;\, -1)$ is the first row.
la-f-matrices-k37 · level facile · notes
Column $j$ of $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{m \times n}$ is denoted by $C_j(\boldsymbol A)$. How many components does $C_j(\boldsymbol A)$ have?
  1. $j$
  2. $m$
  3. $n$
  4. $m\,n$
Explanation. A column has one entry for each row: $m$ components. Rows, instead, have $n$ components.
la-f-matrices-k38 · level facile · notes
$\boldsymbol A_{ij}$ is the complementary submatrix: $\boldsymbol A$ without row $i$ and column $j$. For $\boldsymbol A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & -1 & 0 \\ 3 & 4 & 1 \\ 5 & 0 & 6 \end{pmatrix}$, what is $\det(\boldsymbol A_{31})$?
  1. $-20$
  2. $11$
  3. $1$
  4. $-1$
Explanation. Removing row $3$ and column $1$ leaves $\begin{pmatrix} -1 & 0 \\ 4 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, with determinant $-1$. $-20$ is $\det(\boldsymbol A_{13})$ (indices swapped), $11$ is $\det(\boldsymbol A_{33})$.
la-f-matrices-k39 · level facile · notes
$\boldsymbol A_{ij}$ is the complementary submatrix: $\boldsymbol A$ without row $i$ and column $j$. For $\boldsymbol A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 \\ 4 & 5 & 6 \end{pmatrix}$, for which pair $(i, j)$ do we have $\boldsymbol A_{ij} = (1 \;\; 3)$?
  1. $(2, 3)$
  2. $(2, 2)$
  3. $(1, 2)$
  4. $(2, 1)$
Explanation. To keep the entries $1$ and $3$ of the first row one must remove row $2$ and column $2$. $(1, 2)$ would leave $(4 \;\, 6)$.
la-f-matrices-k40 · level facile · notes
What is the neutral element of matrix addition in $\R^{2\times 3}$, i.e. the matrix $\boldsymbol Z$ with $\boldsymbol A + \boldsymbol Z = \boldsymbol A$ for every $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{2\times 3}$?
  1. The $3 \times 2$ zero matrix
  2. The $2 \times 3$ zero matrix
  3. The identity matrix $\boldsymbol I$
  4. The $2 \times 3$ matrix of all ones
Explanation. Addition is entrywise, so a $2 \times 3$ matrix of zeros is needed. $\boldsymbol I$ is square and is the neutral element of the product, not of the sum.
la-f-matrices-k41 · level facile · notes
For matrices $\boldsymbol A, \boldsymbol B, \boldsymbol C \in \R^{m\times n}$, which equality always holds?
  1. $\boldsymbol A - (\boldsymbol B - \boldsymbol C) = (\boldsymbol A - \boldsymbol B) - \boldsymbol C$
  2. $(\boldsymbol A + \boldsymbol B + \boldsymbol C)' = \boldsymbol A' + \boldsymbol B' - \boldsymbol C'$
  3. $2(\boldsymbol A + \boldsymbol B + \boldsymbol C) = 2\boldsymbol A + \boldsymbol B + \boldsymbol C$
  4. $(\boldsymbol A + \boldsymbol B) + \boldsymbol C = \boldsymbol A + (\boldsymbol B + \boldsymbol C)$
Explanation. Matrix addition is associative (as addition of numbers is, entry by entry). The difference is not associative (on the left $+\boldsymbol C$ appears), the transpose of the sum is the sum of the transposes, and the scalar multiplies all terms.
la-f-matrices-k42 · level facile · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$ and $\boldsymbol B = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$. Which statement about the products is true?
  1. $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B = \boldsymbol 0$ but $\boldsymbol B\boldsymbol A \neq \boldsymbol 0$
  2. $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B = \boldsymbol B\boldsymbol A$
  3. $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B \neq \boldsymbol 0$ but $\boldsymbol B\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol 0$
  4. $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B = \boldsymbol B\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol 0$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$ and $\boldsymbol B\boldsymbol A = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$: the matrix product is not commutative, and it can be zero with nonzero factors.
la-f-matrices-k43 · level facile · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{m\times n}$ and let $\boldsymbol I_k$ be the $k \times k$ identity matrix. Which equality holds?
  1. $\boldsymbol I_m\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol A\,\boldsymbol I_n = \boldsymbol A'$
  2. $\boldsymbol I_m\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol A\,\boldsymbol I_n = \boldsymbol I$
  3. $\boldsymbol I_m\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol A\,\boldsymbol I_n = \boldsymbol A$
  4. $\boldsymbol I_n\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol A\,\boldsymbol I_m = \boldsymbol A$
Explanation. Multiplying on the left needs an identity with as many columns as $\boldsymbol A$ has rows ($m$), on the right one with as many rows as $\boldsymbol A$ has columns ($n$); the result is $\boldsymbol A$.
la-f-matrices-k44 · level facile · notes
A permutation matrix is obtained by reordering the rows of $\boldsymbol I_n$. What is the determinant of the one obtained from $\boldsymbol I_n$ with a single swap of two rows?
  1. $0$
  2. $n$
  3. $-1$
  4. $1$
Explanation. $\det(\boldsymbol I_n) = 1$ and a row swap changes the sign of the determinant: $-1$. It is not $0$, since a permutation matrix is invertible ($\boldsymbol P^{-1} = \boldsymbol P'$).
la-f-matrices-k45 · level facile · notes
A permutation matrix is obtained by reordering the rows of the identity matrix. Which of the following is a permutation matrix?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 0 & -1 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. The first has the rows $\boldsymbol e_3', \boldsymbol e_1', \boldsymbol e_2'$ of the identity: a single $1$ in each row and column. The second repeats a row, the third has a $-1$, the fourth has a zero row.
la-f-matrices-k46 · level facile · notes
With the Laplace expansion (the second column, which has two zeros, is convenient), what is $\det\begin{pmatrix} 3 & 0 & 1 \\ 2 & 0 & 5 \\ 4 & 7 & 6 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. $-91$
  2. $91$
  3. $13$
  4. $0$
Explanation. Only the term $7 \cdot (-1)^{3+2} \det\begin{pmatrix} 3 & 1 \\ 2 & 5 \end{pmatrix} = -7 \cdot 13 = -91$ remains. $91$ forgets the sign $(-1)^{i+j}$; $0$ mistakes a column with two zeros for a zero column.
la-f-matrices-k47 · level facile · notes
In the Laplace expansion the cofactor of entry $(i, j)$ is $C_{ij} = (-1)^{i+j} \det(\boldsymbol A_{ij})$. What is the sign factor $(-1)^{i+j}$ for the entry in position $(2, 3)$?
  1. $1$
  2. $6$
  3. $5$
  4. $-1$
Explanation. $(-1)^{2+3} = (-1)^5 = -1$: the signs alternate like a chessboard, with $+$ in position $(1, 1)$. $5$ is the exponent, not the sign.
la-f-matrices-k48 · level facile · notes
The determinant of a triangular matrix is the product of the diagonal entries. For which $t$ does the matrix $\begin{pmatrix} 2 & 5 & 1 \\ 0 & t - 1 & 3 \\ 0 & 0 & 4 \end{pmatrix}$ have zero determinant?
  1. $t = 3$
  2. $t = 1$
  3. $t = 0$
  4. $t = -1$
Explanation. $\det = 2\,(t - 1) \cdot 4 = 8\,(t - 1)$, zero for $t = 1$. The entries above the diagonal ($5$, $1$, $3$) do not count.
la-f-matrices-k49 · level facile · notes
The identity matrix $\boldsymbol I_n$ is diagonal, with all diagonal entries equal to $1$. What is $\det(\boldsymbol I_n)$?
  1. $0$
  2. $n!$
  3. $1$
  4. $n$
Explanation. The determinant of a diagonal matrix is the product of the diagonal entries: $1^n = 1$. $n$ adds them instead of multiplying.
la-f-matrices-k50 · level facile · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{3\times 3}$ with $\det(\boldsymbol A) = -2$ and let $\boldsymbol A'$ be its transpose. What is $\det(\boldsymbol A')$?
  1. $2$
  2. $-\frac12$
  3. $-8$
  4. $-2$
Explanation. Transposition does not change the determinant: $\det(\boldsymbol A') = \det(\boldsymbol A) = -2$. $-\frac12$ is the determinant of the inverse, $-8 = (-2)^3$.
la-f-matrices-k51 · level facile · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A, \boldsymbol B \in \R^{2\times 2}$ with $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 3$ and $\det(\boldsymbol B) = -2$. What is $\det(\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B)$?
  1. $1$
  2. $6$
  3. $-24$
  4. $-6$
Explanation. The determinant is multiplicative: $\det(\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B) = \det(\boldsymbol A)\det(\boldsymbol B) = -6$. $1 = 3 + (-2)$ treats it as additive.

4.2. Matrix operations (26)

la-f-elem-01 · level facile · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$ and let $R_i$ be its row $i$. How does $\det(\boldsymbol A)$ change with the row swap $R_1 \leftrightarrow R_2$?
  1. it does not change
  2. it doubles
  3. it becomes $0$
  4. it changes sign
Explanation. Swapping two rows changes the sign of the determinant: $\det(\text{new}) = -\det(\boldsymbol A)$.
la-f-elem-02 · level facile · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$ and let $R_i$ be its row $i$. How does $\det(\boldsymbol A)$ change with the operation $R_2 \leftarrow R_2 - 4R_1$ (from row $2$, $4$ times row $1$ is subtracted)?
  1. it changes sign
  2. it does not change
  3. it is multiplied by $-4$
  4. it is multiplied by $4$
Explanation. Adding to a row a multiple of another row does not change the determinant: it is the operation used in Gaussian elimination.
la-f-elem-03 · level facile · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$. How does $\det(\boldsymbol A)$ change with the operation $R_i \leftarrow \lambda R_i$, which multiplies row $i$ by $\lambda \neq 0$?
  1. it is multiplied by $1/\lambda$
  2. it is multiplied by $\lambda$
  3. it is multiplied by $\lambda^n$
  4. it does not change
Explanation. Only one row is multiplied, so the determinant is multiplied by $\lambda$; $\lambda^n$ applies when the whole matrix is multiplied ($\lambda\boldsymbol A$).
la-f-elem-04 · level facile · notes
The pivot of a nonzero row is its first nonzero entry. A matrix $\boldsymbol U \in \R^{m\times n}$ is in row echelon form if:
  1. each row has only one nonzero entry, its pivot, and the zero rows are at the bottom
  2. the zero rows are at the bottom and the pivot of each row is strictly to the right of the one of the row above
  3. the zero rows are at the bottom and each row has at least one more zero entry than the row above
  4. it is upper triangular and all its diagonal entries are nonzero
Explanation. These are the two conditions of the definition; it follows that below each pivot there are only zeros. A matrix in echelon form need not be square, and it may have zero rows.
la-f-elem-05 · level facile · notes
In Gaussian elimination the pivot is $a_{kj}$ (row $k$, column $j$) and $R_i$ denotes row $i$. Which operation cancels the entry $a_{ij}$ of a row $i$ below the pivot?
  1. $R_i \leftarrow a_{kj} R_i - R_k$
  2. $R_i \leftarrow R_i - \frac{a_{ij}}{a_{kj}}\, R_k$
  3. $R_i \leftarrow R_i - \frac{a_{kj}}{a_{ij}}\, R_k$
  4. $R_i \leftarrow R_i + \frac{a_{ij}}{a_{kj}}\, R_k$
Explanation. After the operation the entry in position $(i,j)$ is $a_{ij} - \frac{a_{ij}}{a_{kj}}a_{kj} = 0$. The last one multiplies row $i$ and would change the determinant.
la-f-elem-06 · level teorema · notes
Let $\boldsymbol U$ be the echelon form, with diagonal entries $u_{11}, \dots, u_{nn}$, obtained by Gaussian elimination on $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$ with $s$ row swaps and additions of multiples of rows (no row multiplied by a scalar). Which formula is correct?
  1. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = (-1)^{s}\prod_{i=1}^{n} u_{ii}$
  2. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = \prod_{i=1}^{n} u_{ii}$
  3. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = (-1)^{n}\prod_{i=1}^{n} u_{ii}$
  4. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = (-1)^{s}\sum_{i=1}^{n} u_{ii}$
Explanation. Row additions do not change the determinant, each swap changes the sign, and $\boldsymbol U$ is triangular. In particular $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 0$ if and only if $\boldsymbol U$ has a zero on its diagonal.
la-f-elem-07 · level facile · notes
Gaussian elimination on $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{3\times 3}$ uses $2$ row swaps and additions of multiples of rows (no row multiplied by a scalar) and gives an echelon form with diagonal $1, -2, 3$. What is $\det(\boldsymbol A)$?
  1. $0$
  2. $-6$
  3. $6$
  4. $2$
Explanation. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = (-1)^{2}\cdot 1\cdot(-2)\cdot 3 = -6$: two swaps leave the sign unchanged.
la-f-elem-08 · level facile · notes
Gaussian elimination on $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$ with partial pivoting: at step $k$, by a row swap, which entry of column $k$ is brought to position $(k,k)$?
  1. the first nonzero entry among $a_{kk}, \dots, a_{nk}$
  2. the nonzero entry of smallest absolute value among $a_{kk}, \dots, a_{nk}$
  3. the entry of largest absolute value among $a_{kk}, a_{k+1,k}, \dots, a_{nk}$
  4. the largest entry (with its sign) among $a_{kk}, \dots, a_{nk}$
Explanation. The pivot with largest absolute value is chosen: the multipliers have modulus $\le 1$ and round-off errors are reduced. The first nonzero one is plain pivoting.
la-f-elem-09 · level facile · notes
The first column of $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{3\times 3}$ is $(1,\, 3,\, -4)'$. With partial pivoting, which row becomes the first one at the first step?
  1. row $2$
  2. row $1$ (no swap)
  3. none: columns are swapped
  4. row $3$
Explanation. The absolute values $1, 3, 4$ are compared: the maximum is $|-4| = 4$, in row $3$, hence $R_1 \leftrightarrow R_3$.
la-f-elem-10 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$. Elementary column operations (swap, multiplication by $\lambda \neq 0$, addition of a multiple of another column) have the same effect on the determinant as the row operations. Which result guarantees it?
  1. the Sarrus rule: its six products treat rows and columns in the same way
  2. $\det(\boldsymbol A') = \det(\boldsymbol A)$: a column operation on $\boldsymbol A$ is a row operation on $\boldsymbol A'$
  3. $\det(\lambda\boldsymbol A) = \lambda^n\det(\boldsymbol A)$: the $n$ columns contribute as the $n$ rows
  4. $\det(\boldsymbol A^{-1}) = 1/\det(\boldsymbol A)$: a column operation on $\boldsymbol A$ is a row operation on $\boldsymbol A^{-1}$
Explanation. The transpose interchanges rows and columns without changing the determinant: operating on the columns of $\boldsymbol A$ is operating on the rows of $\boldsymbol A'$. Sarrus holds only for $n = 3$.
la-f-elem-11 · level ipotesi · notes
Gaussian elimination on $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$, with $s$ row swaps and additions of multiples of rows, gives the echelon form $\boldsymbol U$ (diagonal entries $u_{ii}$). Which results are needed to prove $\det(\boldsymbol A) = (-1)^{s}\prod_{i} u_{ii}$?
  1. $\det(\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B) = \det(\boldsymbol A)\det(\boldsymbol B)$; $\det(\boldsymbol A^{-1}) = 1/\det(\boldsymbol A)$; Cramer's rule
  2. effect of swaps and of adding row multiples; $\det$ of a triangular matrix
  3. effect of multiplying a row by $\lambda$; $\det(\lambda\boldsymbol A) = \lambda^n\det(\boldsymbol A)$; Laplace expansion
  4. effect on the determinant of the row swap; $\det(\boldsymbol A') = \det(\boldsymbol A)$; Sarrus rule
Explanation. Each swap changes the sign ($s$ times), adding a multiple of another row does not change the determinant, and $\boldsymbol U$ is upper triangular, hence $\det(\boldsymbol U) = \prod_i u_{ii}$.
la-f-elem-12 · level facile · notes
Which of these is an elementary row operation?
  1. $R_i \leftarrow R_i\, R_k$ (entrywise product)
  2. $R_i \leftarrow \lambda R_i$ with $\lambda \neq 0$
  3. $R_i \leftarrow \lambda R_i$ with any $\lambda \in \R$
  4. $R_i \leftarrow R_i + \lambda R_i$ with $\lambda = -1$
Explanation. The scalar must be nonzero: with $\lambda = 0$ (or $R_i \leftarrow R_i - R_i$) the row vanishes and the operation is not invertible. Row addition uses another row $R_k$, with $k \neq i$.
la-f-elem-13 · level facile · notes
Let $R_i$ be row $i$. Applying the operation $R_2 \leftarrow R_2 + 2R_1$ to the matrix $\boldsymbol A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 3 \\ -2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ gives:
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} -3 & 5 \\ -2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 3 \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 3 \\ 0 & 7 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 3 \\ -4 & -5 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. Only row $2$ changes: $(-2 + 2\cdot 1,\; 1 + 2\cdot 3) = (0,\; 7)$. Subtracting instead of adding gives $(-4,\; -5)$; $\begin{pmatrix} -3 & 5 \\ -2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ is $R_1 \leftarrow R_1 + 2R_2$.
la-f-elem-k01 · level facile · notes
What are the three elementary row operations on a matrix?
  1. Transpose, invert, multiply by $\boldsymbol A$
  2. Add two columns, delete a row, double the matrix
  3. Square, change sign, sort the rows
  4. Swap, scaling by $\alpha \neq 0$, adding a multiple
Explanation. They are the operations of Gaussian elimination.
la-f-elem-k02 · level facile · notes
An elementary row operation is equivalent to multiplying $\boldsymbol A$:
  1. on the right by an elementary matrix
  2. by a nonzero scalar
  3. on the right by its transpose
  4. on the left by an elementary matrix
Explanation. Column operations correspond to right multiplication.
la-f-elem-k03 · level facile · notes
Gaussian elimination on $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$ (row swaps and additions of multiples of rows) produces an echelon form $\boldsymbol U$ with a zero diagonal entry. What can be concluded?
  1. $\det(\boldsymbol A) < 0$
  2. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 1$
  3. Nothing about $\det(\boldsymbol A)$
  4. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 0$
Explanation. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = \pm \prod_i u_{ii}$ (sign $(-1)^s$, with $s$ swaps), and the product contains a zero factor: $\boldsymbol A$ is singular.
la-f-elem-k04 · level facile · notes
The elementary row operations are swapping two rows, multiplying a row by a scalar and adding to a row a multiple of another one. Which operation is not allowed?
  1. Swapping two rows
  2. Adding to a row $3$ times another one
  3. Multiplying a row by $-2$
  4. Multiplying a row by $0$
Explanation. The scalar must be nonzero, otherwise the operation is not invertible (the information of the row is lost and the determinant becomes $0$). The others are elementary operations.
la-f-elem-k05 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about computing the determinant by Gaussian elimination is correct?
  1. The product of the pivots, times $(-1)^s$
  2. It is the sum of the pivots
  3. It is the product of the pivots, always with positive sign
  4. It cannot be obtained from elimination
Explanation. The echelon form is triangular; only swaps change the sign.
la-f-elem-k06 · level facile · notes
In Gaussian elimination, when is pivoting (a row swap) needed at step $k$?
  1. When $a_{kk}$ is positive
  2. Only at the last step
  3. When $a_{kk} = 0$
  4. At every step, always
Explanation. Division by zero is impossible: another row with a nonzero entry is chosen.
la-f-elem-k07 · level facile · notes
What is the pivot entry in Gaussian elimination?
  1. The determinant of the matrix
  2. The divisor of elimination
  3. The largest entry of the matrix
  4. The last diagonal entry
Explanation. At step $k$ it is the entry in position $(k, k)$ after possible swaps.
la-f-elem-k08 · level facile · notes
If at step $k$ of elimination the whole column $k$ from row $k$ down is zero, what is concluded for a square matrix?
  1. The matrix is invertible
  2. An error was made
  3. The determinant is $1$
  4. The matrix is singular
Explanation. There is no nonzero pivot: the determinant is $0$.
la-f-elem-k09 · level facile · notes
With partial pivoting the pivot row is the one with the entry of largest absolute value in the column, from the row of the step downwards. At the second step on $\begin{pmatrix} 4 & 1 & 2 \\ 0 & 1 & 3 \\ 0 & -5 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, which row becomes the pivot row?
  1. The third
  2. The second
  3. The first
  4. None
Explanation. In column $2$, from rows $2$ and $3$, the candidates are $1$ and $-5$: $|-5| > |1|$, so rows $2$ and $3$ are swapped. The first row no longer takes part.
la-f-elem-k10 · level ipotesi · notes
Why does partial pivoting improve the numerical stability of Gaussian elimination?
  1. It makes the reduced matrix symmetric
  2. It avoids all rounding errors
  3. It reduces the total number of operations
  4. The multipliers have absolute value at most $1$
Explanation. Dividing by the largest pivot keeps the multipliers small.
la-f-elem-k11 · level ipotesi · notes
Why do elementary row operations not change the solution set of $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ (applied to the augmented matrix)?
  1. Because they do not change the determinant
  2. Because they leave $\boldsymbol b$ unchanged
  3. Because $\boldsymbol A$ is square
  4. Because they are invertible
Explanation. Each operation has an inverse of the same type.
la-f-elem-k12 · level facile · notes
In row elimination on $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$, at step $k$ the pivot $a_{kk}$ is zero. Which row can row $k$ be swapped with?
  1. With a row $i < k$ with $a_{ik} \neq 0$
  2. With a row $i > k$ with $a_{ki} \neq 0$
  3. With any row $i > k$
  4. With a row $i > k$ with $a_{ik} \neq 0$
Explanation. A nonzero entry is needed in column $k$, below the pivot: the rows above already have their pivots, and a row with $a_{ik} = 0$ does not solve the problem.
la-f-elem-k13 · level facile · notes
After the first step of Gaussian elimination on a $3 \times 3$ matrix one has $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 \\ 0 & 0 & 4 \\ 0 & 5 & 6 \end{pmatrix}$ ($R_i$: row $i$). What is done at the second step?
  1. One concludes that the matrix is singular
  2. Rows $R_1$ and $R_2$ are swapped
  3. $R_3$ is replaced by $R_3 - R_2$
  4. Rows $R_2$ and $R_3$ are swapped
Explanation. The pivot $a_{22}$ is zero but $a_{32} = 5 \neq 0$: the swap $R_2 \leftrightarrow R_3$ already gives a triangular matrix. The matrix is not singular (its determinant is $-20$).

4.3. Inversion of matrices (21)

la-f-inv-01 · level facile · notes
If $\boldsymbol A, \boldsymbol B \in \R^{n\times n}$ are invertible, what is $(\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B)^{-1}$?
  1. $(\boldsymbol B\boldsymbol A)'$
  2. $\boldsymbol B^{-1}\boldsymbol A^{-1}$
  3. $\boldsymbol A^{-1}\boldsymbol B^{-1}$
  4. $\boldsymbol A^{-1} + \boldsymbol B^{-1}$
Explanation. The order is reversed: $(\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B)(\boldsymbol B^{-1}\boldsymbol A^{-1}) = \boldsymbol A\boldsymbol I\boldsymbol A^{-1} = \boldsymbol I$. Since the product is not commutative, in general $(\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B)^{-1} \neq \boldsymbol A^{-1}\boldsymbol B^{-1}$.
la-f-inv-02 · level facile · notes
If $\boldsymbol A$ is invertible, what is $(\boldsymbol A')^{-1}$?
  1. $-(\boldsymbol A^{-1})'$
  2. $\boldsymbol A$
  3. $(\boldsymbol A^{-1})'$
  4. $\boldsymbol A^{-1}$
Explanation. Inverse and transpose commute: $\boldsymbol A'(\boldsymbol A^{-1})' = (\boldsymbol A^{-1}\boldsymbol A)' = \boldsymbol I' = \boldsymbol I$. $\boldsymbol A^{-1}$ is right only if $\boldsymbol A$ is symmetric.
la-f-inv-03 · level facile · notes
What is the inverse of $\boldsymbol A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & -1 \\ -1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 & -1 \\ -1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 & 1 \\ 1 & -2 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 1$: swap the diagonal entries, change the sign of the off-diagonal ones and divide by $1$. Check: $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol A^{-1} = \boldsymbol I$.
la-f-inv-04 · level facile · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$ and let $\boldsymbol C$ be the cofactor matrix, with $C_{ij} = (-1)^{i+j}\det(\boldsymbol A_{ij})$, where $\boldsymbol A_{ij}$ is obtained by deleting row $i$ and column $j$. The adjugate matrix $\mathrm{adj}(\boldsymbol A)$ is:
  1. $\frac{1}{\det(\boldsymbol A)}\boldsymbol C$
  2. $\boldsymbol A'$
  3. $\boldsymbol C'$
  4. $\boldsymbol C$
Explanation. The adjugate is the transpose of the cofactor matrix: $(\mathrm{adj}(\boldsymbol A))_{ij} = C_{ji}$. Dividing it by $\det(\boldsymbol A)$ gives $\boldsymbol A^{-1}$.
la-f-inv-05 · level teorema · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$, $n \ge 2$, and let $\mathrm{adj}(\boldsymbol A)$ be its adjugate matrix (the transpose of the cofactor matrix). Which equality always holds?
  1. $\boldsymbol A\,\mathrm{adj}(\boldsymbol A) = \det(\boldsymbol A)\,\boldsymbol I$
  2. $\boldsymbol A\,\mathrm{adj}(\boldsymbol A) = \boldsymbol I$
  3. $\mathrm{adj}(\boldsymbol A)\,\boldsymbol A = \det(\boldsymbol A)\,\boldsymbol A$
  4. $\boldsymbol A\,\mathrm{adj}(\boldsymbol A) = \det(\boldsymbol A)\,\boldsymbol A'$
Explanation. It is the adjugate formula, valid also when $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 0$; if $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$ it gives $\boldsymbol A^{-1} = \frac{1}{\det(\boldsymbol A)}\mathrm{adj}(\boldsymbol A)$.
la-f-inv-06 · level facile · notes
In the Gauss–Jordan method one starts from $(\boldsymbol A \mid \boldsymbol I)$ and, with elementary row operations, reaches $(\boldsymbol I \mid \boldsymbol B)$. Then:
  1. $\boldsymbol B = \det(\boldsymbol A)\,\boldsymbol A^{-1}$
  2. $\boldsymbol B = \boldsymbol A$
  3. $\boldsymbol B = \boldsymbol A^{-1}$
  4. $\boldsymbol B = \boldsymbol A'$
Explanation. The operations transforming $\boldsymbol A$ into $\boldsymbol I$, applied to $\boldsymbol I$, produce $\boldsymbol A^{-1}$.
la-f-inv-07 · level facile · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$. While applying the Gauss–Jordan method to $(\boldsymbol A \mid \boldsymbol I)$ a zero row appears in the left block. What can be concluded?
  1. $\boldsymbol A$ is invertible, but a column swap is needed
  2. $\boldsymbol A$ is invertible only if the right block has the same zero row
  3. $\boldsymbol A$ is singular: $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 0$ and $\boldsymbol A^{-1}$ does not exist
  4. $\boldsymbol A^{-1}$ exists and has a zero row in the same position
Explanation. Elementary operations do not change whether the determinant vanishes: a zero row in the left block means zero determinant, and the method stops.
la-f-inv-08 · level teorema · notes
Which characterization of invertible matrices $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$ is correct?
  1. $\boldsymbol A$ is invertible if and only if it is symmetric
  2. $\boldsymbol A$ is invertible if and only if all its entries are nonzero
  3. $\boldsymbol A$ is invertible if and only if $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$
  4. $\boldsymbol A$ is invertible if and only if $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 1$
Explanation. If $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$ the inverse is $\frac{1}{\det(\boldsymbol A)}\mathrm{adj}(\boldsymbol A)$; if $\boldsymbol A$ is invertible, $\det(\boldsymbol A)\det(\boldsymbol A^{-1}) = 1$ forces $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$.
la-f-inv-09 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A, \boldsymbol B, \boldsymbol C \in \R^{n\times n}$ with $\boldsymbol B\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol I$ and $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol C = \boldsymbol I$. Which property is needed to prove that $\boldsymbol B = \boldsymbol C$ (uniqueness of the inverse)?
  1. the associativity of the matrix product
  2. the commutativity of the matrix product
  3. the rule $\det(\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B) = \det(\boldsymbol A)\det(\boldsymbol B)$
  4. the distributivity of the product over the sum
Explanation. $\boldsymbol B = \boldsymbol B\boldsymbol I = \boldsymbol B(\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol C) = (\boldsymbol B\boldsymbol A)\boldsymbol C = \boldsymbol I\boldsymbol C = \boldsymbol C$. The matrix product is not commutative, and determinants only give $\det(\boldsymbol B) = \det(\boldsymbol C)$.
la-f-inv-10 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A, \boldsymbol B \in \R^{n\times n}$ be invertible. Which results are needed to prove that $(\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B)^{-1} = \boldsymbol B^{-1}\boldsymbol A^{-1}$?
  1. the associativity of the product and the uniqueness of the inverse
  2. the commutativity of the product and the uniqueness of the inverse
  3. the rule $\det(\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B) = \det(\boldsymbol A)\det(\boldsymbol B)$ and Cramer's rule
  4. the rule $(\boldsymbol C\boldsymbol D)' = \boldsymbol D'\boldsymbol C'$ and the associativity of the product
Explanation. $(\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B)(\boldsymbol B^{-1}\boldsymbol A^{-1}) = \boldsymbol A(\boldsymbol B\boldsymbol B^{-1})\boldsymbol A^{-1} = \boldsymbol I$ and similarly in the other order; by uniqueness $\boldsymbol B^{-1}\boldsymbol A^{-1}$ is the inverse of $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B$. Without commutativity the order of the factors is reversed.
la-f-inv-11 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$ be invertible and let $'$ denote the transpose. Which results are needed to prove that $(\boldsymbol A')^{-1} = (\boldsymbol A^{-1})'$?
  1. $(\boldsymbol C\boldsymbol D)' = \boldsymbol D'\boldsymbol C'$, $\boldsymbol I' = \boldsymbol I$ and the uniqueness of the inverse
  2. $(\boldsymbol C\boldsymbol D)' = \boldsymbol C'\boldsymbol D'$, $\boldsymbol I' = \boldsymbol I$ and the uniqueness of the inverse
  3. $\det(\boldsymbol A') = \det(\boldsymbol A)$ and $\det(\boldsymbol A^{-1}) = 1/\det(\boldsymbol A)$
  4. the symmetry of $\boldsymbol A$, $\boldsymbol I' = \boldsymbol I$ and the uniqueness of the inverse
Explanation. $\boldsymbol A'(\boldsymbol A^{-1})' = (\boldsymbol A^{-1}\boldsymbol A)' = \boldsymbol I' = \boldsymbol I$, and similarly in the other order: by uniqueness $(\boldsymbol A^{-1})'$ is the inverse of $\boldsymbol A'$. The transpose of a product reverses the order of the factors; $\boldsymbol A$ need not be symmetric.
la-f-inv-12 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $\boldsymbol C$ be the cofactor matrix of $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$, so $\mathrm{adj}(\boldsymbol A) = \boldsymbol C'$ and entry $(i,j)$ of $\boldsymbol A\,\mathrm{adj}(\boldsymbol A)$ is $\sum_k a_{ik}C_{jk}$. Why is this entry zero for $i \neq j$?
  1. it is the scalar product of two distinct rows of $\boldsymbol A$, zero if $\boldsymbol A$ is symmetric
  2. it follows from $\det(\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B) = \det(\boldsymbol A)\det(\boldsymbol B)$ with $\boldsymbol B = \mathrm{adj}(\boldsymbol A)$
  3. it is the Laplace expansion of a matrix with two equal rows
  4. the cofactors $C_{jk}$ with $j \neq i$ are zero for every $k$
Explanation. $\sum_k a_{ik}C_{jk}$ is the expansion along row $j$ of the matrix obtained from $\boldsymbol A$ by putting row $i$ in place of row $j$: it has two equal rows, hence determinant $0$. For $i = j$ it is the expansion of $\det(\boldsymbol A)$.
la-f-inv-13 · level facile · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A$ be invertible with $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 4$. What is $\det(\boldsymbol A^{-1})$?
  1. $\frac{1}{4}$
  2. $4$
  3. $-4$
  4. $\frac{1}{16}$
Explanation. $\det(\boldsymbol A)\det(\boldsymbol A^{-1}) = \det(\boldsymbol I) = 1$, hence $\det(\boldsymbol A^{-1}) = \frac{1}{4}$.
la-f-inverse-k01 · level facile · notes
In the Gauss–Jordan method for the inverse of $\boldsymbol A$, on which matrix does one operate and what is obtained?
  1. On $\boldsymbol A'$, up to $\boldsymbol I$
  2. On $(\boldsymbol I \,|\, \boldsymbol A)$, up to $(\boldsymbol A \,|\, \boldsymbol I)$
  3. On $(\boldsymbol A \,|\, \boldsymbol I)$, up to $(\boldsymbol I \,|\, \boldsymbol A^{-1})$
  4. On $(\boldsymbol A \,|\, \boldsymbol b)$, up to $(\boldsymbol I \,|\, \boldsymbol x)$
Explanation. The same operations turn $\boldsymbol I$ into $\boldsymbol A^{-1}$.
la-f-inverse-k02 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the inverse of a square matrix is correct?
  1. It equals the transpose
  2. If it exists, it is unique
  3. Two different inverses can exist
  4. It always exists
Explanation. If $\boldsymbol B$ and $\boldsymbol C$ are inverses, $\boldsymbol B = \boldsymbol B(\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol C) = (\boldsymbol B\boldsymbol A)\boldsymbol C = \boldsymbol C$.
la-f-inverse-k03 · level ipotesi · notes
The formula $\boldsymbol A^{-1} = \frac{1}{\det(\boldsymbol A)}\,\mathrm{adj}(\boldsymbol A)$ holds under which assumption?
  1. $\boldsymbol A$ triangular
  2. $\det(\boldsymbol A) > 0$
  3. $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$
  4. $\boldsymbol A$ symmetric
Explanation. Division by the determinant is needed.
la-f-inverse-k04 · level facile · notes
If $\boldsymbol A$ is invertible, what is $(\boldsymbol A^{-1})^{-1}$?
  1. $\boldsymbol A'$
  2. $\boldsymbol A$
  3. $\boldsymbol A^{-1}$
  4. $\boldsymbol I$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol A$ satisfies the definition of inverse of $\boldsymbol A^{-1}$, which is unique.
la-f-inverse-k05 · level ipotesi · notes
In the proof of uniqueness of the inverse, from $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B = \boldsymbol I$ and $\boldsymbol C\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol I$ one concludes $\boldsymbol B = \boldsymbol C$. Which property of the product is used?
  1. Distributivity over addition
  2. That $\boldsymbol A$ is a symmetric matrix
  3. Associativity: $\boldsymbol C(\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B) = (\boldsymbol C\boldsymbol A)\boldsymbol B$
  4. Commutativity: $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B = \boldsymbol B\boldsymbol A$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol C = \boldsymbol C\boldsymbol I = \boldsymbol C(\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B) = (\boldsymbol C\boldsymbol A)\boldsymbol B = \boldsymbol B$.
la-f-inverse-k06 · level facile · notes
With the adjugate matrix, what is the inverse of $\begin{pmatrix} 3 & 2 \\ 1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & -2 \\ -1 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 1 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 3 & -2 \\ -1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 & 2 \\ 1 & -3 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. $\det = 1$ and $\mathrm{adj} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & -2 \\ -1 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$.
la-f-inverse-k07 · level facile · notes
With the Gauss–Jordan method $(\boldsymbol A \mid \boldsymbol I)$ is transformed into $(\boldsymbol I \mid \boldsymbol A^{-1})$. For $\boldsymbol A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 4 \end{pmatrix}$, what appears at the end in the right block?
  1. $\mathrm{diag}(-2,\, -4)$
  2. $\mathrm{diag}(\frac14,\, \frac12)$
  3. $\mathrm{diag}(\frac12,\, \frac14)$
  4. $\mathrm{diag}(2,\, 4)$
Explanation. It suffices to divide the first row by $2$ and the second by $4$: the right block becomes $\mathrm{diag}(\frac12, \frac14) = \boldsymbol A^{-1}$. The inverses do not swap places.
la-f-inverse-k08 · level facile · notes
In the Gauss–Jordan method for the inverse one works on the matrix $(\boldsymbol A \mid \boldsymbol I)$. Which operations are allowed?
  1. Elementary row operations, on both blocks
  2. Elementary row operations, on the block $\boldsymbol A$ only
  3. Column operations, on the block $\boldsymbol I$ only
  4. Any operation that leaves the determinant unchanged
Explanation. Each row operation must be applied to the whole row of $(\boldsymbol A \mid \boldsymbol I)$: it is like multiplying both blocks on the left by the same elementary matrix, and at the end the right block is $\boldsymbol A^{-1}$.

4.4. Factorization of matrices (15)

la-f-lu-01 · level facile · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$. In the factorization $\boldsymbol P\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$ computed by Gaussian elimination, which matrices are $\boldsymbol P$, $\boldsymbol L$ and $\boldsymbol U$?
  1. $\boldsymbol P = \boldsymbol I$ always, $\boldsymbol L$ lower triangular with ones on the diagonal, $\boldsymbol U$ upper triangular
  2. $\boldsymbol P$ a permutation matrix, $\boldsymbol L$ lower triangular with ones on the diagonal, $\boldsymbol U$ upper triangular
  3. $\boldsymbol P$ a permutation matrix, $\boldsymbol L$ lower triangular, $\boldsymbol U$ upper triangular with ones on the diagonal
  4. $\boldsymbol P$ diagonal, $\boldsymbol L$ upper triangular with ones on the diagonal, $\boldsymbol U$ lower triangular
Explanation. $\boldsymbol P$ records the row swaps (it is $\boldsymbol I$ only if no swap is needed), $\boldsymbol L$ stores the multipliers below a diagonal of ones, $\boldsymbol U$ is the echelon form, upper triangular.
la-f-lu-02 · level facile · notes
$\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$ is computed by Gaussian elimination; $R_i$ denotes row $i$. Where does the multiplier $\ell_{ij}$ of the step $R_i \leftarrow R_i - \ell_{ij}R_j$ ($i > j$), which cancels position $(i,j)$, go?
  1. in position $(i,j)$ of $\boldsymbol L$, with the opposite sign
  2. on the diagonal of $\boldsymbol L$
  3. in position $(i,j)$ of $\boldsymbol L$
  4. in position $(j,i)$ of $\boldsymbol U$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol L$ stores the multipliers as they appear in the operation $R_i \leftarrow R_i - \ell_{ij}R_j$; the diagonal of $\boldsymbol L$ is made of ones.
la-f-lu-03 · level teorema · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$ be nonsingular and let $\boldsymbol A_{[k]}$ be the submatrix formed by its first $k$ rows and columns. When does $\boldsymbol A$ admit $\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$ ($\boldsymbol L$ lower triangular with ones on the diagonal, $\boldsymbol U$ upper triangular)?
  1. if and only if $\det(\boldsymbol A_{[k]}) \neq 0$ for every $k \in \{1, \dots, n\}$
  2. always, because $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$
  3. if and only if $\boldsymbol A$ is symmetric
  4. if and only if all diagonal entries of $\boldsymbol A$ are nonzero
Explanation. All leading principal minors must be nonzero: then the pivots $u_{kk} = \det(\boldsymbol A_{[k]})/\det(\boldsymbol A_{[k-1]})$ are nonzero. For instance $\begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ is nonsingular but needs a swap.
la-f-lu-04 · level facile · notes
Does the matrix $\boldsymbol A = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ admit a factorization $\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$ without row swaps ($\boldsymbol L$ lower triangular with ones on the diagonal, $\boldsymbol U$ upper triangular)?
  1. no: the pivot $a_{11}$ is zero
  2. yes, because $\det(\boldsymbol A) = -1 \neq 0$
  3. yes, with $\boldsymbol L = \boldsymbol I$ and $\boldsymbol U = \boldsymbol A$
  4. no, because $\boldsymbol A$ is singular
Explanation. The first leading minor is $\det(\boldsymbol A_{[1]}) = 0$; swapping the rows gives $\boldsymbol P\boldsymbol A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, already upper triangular. $\boldsymbol A$ is not upper triangular, so $\boldsymbol U = \boldsymbol A$ does not work.
la-f-lu-05 · level facile · notes
For $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{3\times 3}$ the leading principal minors ($\boldsymbol A_{[k]}$: first $k$ rows and columns) are $\det(\boldsymbol A_{[1]}) = 2$, $\det(\boldsymbol A_{[2]}) = -16$, $\det(\boldsymbol A) = -16$. What are the pivots $u_{11}, u_{22}, u_{33}$ of the factorization $\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$ without swaps?
  1. $2,\ -16,\ -16$
  2. $2,\ -8,\ 2$
  3. $2,\ -14,\ 0$
  4. $2,\ -8,\ 1$
Explanation. $u_{11} = \det(\boldsymbol A_{[1]})$ and $u_{kk} = \det(\boldsymbol A_{[k]})/\det(\boldsymbol A_{[k-1]})$: $-16/2 = -8$ and $-16/(-16) = 1$. Check: $2\cdot(-8)\cdot 1 = \det(\boldsymbol A)$.
la-f-lu-06 · level teorema · notes
Let $\boldsymbol P\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$ be a PLU factorization of $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$, with $\boldsymbol P$ obtained from $\boldsymbol I_n$ with $s$ row swaps and $u_{ii}$, $\ell_{ii}$ the diagonal entries of $\boldsymbol U$ and $\boldsymbol L$. Which formula is correct?
  1. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = (-1)^{s}\prod_{i=1}^{n} \ell_{ii}$
  2. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = (-1)^{n}\prod_{i=1}^{n} u_{ii}$
  3. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = (-1)^{s}\prod_{i=1}^{n} u_{ii}$
  4. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = \prod_{i=1}^{n} u_{ii}$, whatever $s$ is
Explanation. $\det(\boldsymbol P) = (-1)^s$, $\det(\boldsymbol L) = 1$, $\det(\boldsymbol U) = \prod_i u_{ii}$; hence $(-1)^s\det(\boldsymbol A) = \prod_i u_{ii}$. The product of the $\ell_{ii}$ is always $1$.
la-f-lu-07 · level facile · notes
$\boldsymbol P\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$ is computed by Gaussian elimination and at step $j$ rows $i$ and $k$ are swapped. What happens to the multipliers already stored in $\boldsymbol L$?
  1. those of rows $i$ and $k$ change sign, because the swap changes the sign of the determinant
  2. those of rows $i$ and $k$ (columns $1, \dots, j-1$) are swapped, and so are rows $i$ and $k$ of $\boldsymbol P$
  3. columns $i$ and $k$ of $\boldsymbol L$ are swapped, and so are columns $i$ and $k$ of $\boldsymbol P$
  4. they stay where they are: the swap is applied only to the rows of $\boldsymbol U$ and $\boldsymbol P$
Explanation. Without swapping the multipliers one gets a matrix $\widetilde{\boldsymbol L}$ with $\widetilde{\boldsymbol L}\boldsymbol U \neq \boldsymbol P\boldsymbol A$: it is the mistake pointed out in the notes.
la-f-lu-08 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $\boldsymbol P\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$, with $\boldsymbol P$ obtained from $\boldsymbol I_n$ with $s$ row swaps and $u_{ii}$ the diagonal entries of $\boldsymbol U$. Which results are needed to prove that $\det(\boldsymbol A) = (-1)^s\prod_i u_{ii}$?
  1. Sylvester's criterion on the leading principal minors of $\boldsymbol A$ and $\det(\boldsymbol L) = 1$
  2. $\det(\boldsymbol P + \boldsymbol A) = \det(\boldsymbol P) + \det(\boldsymbol A)$ and the determinant of triangular matrices
  3. the product rule $\det(\boldsymbol P\boldsymbol A) = \det(\boldsymbol P)\det(\boldsymbol A)$, $\det(\boldsymbol P) = (-1)^s$ and the determinant of triangular matrices
  4. $\det(\boldsymbol A') = \det(\boldsymbol A)$, $\det(\boldsymbol P) = (-1)^s$ and the Laplace expansion along the first column of $\boldsymbol A$
Explanation. $\det(\boldsymbol P)\det(\boldsymbol A) = \det(\boldsymbol L)\det(\boldsymbol U)$ with $\det(\boldsymbol P) = (-1)^s$, $\det(\boldsymbol L) = 1$ and $\det(\boldsymbol U) = \prod_i u_{ii}$ (triangular matrices); then multiply by $(-1)^s$. The determinant is not additive.
la-f-lu-09 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$ have all leading principal minors $\det(\boldsymbol A_{[k]})$ nonzero ($\boldsymbol A_{[k]}$: first $k$ rows and columns). Which fact is essential to prove that Gaussian elimination needs no row swap?
  1. $\boldsymbol A$ is symmetric, hence the pivots of the elimination coincide with the eigenvalues
  2. the formula $\det(\lambda\boldsymbol A) = \lambda^n\det(\boldsymbol A)$ applied to each submatrix $\boldsymbol A_{[k]}$
  3. the operations $R_i \leftarrow R_i - \ell_{ij}R_j$ with $j < i$ do not change the minors $\det(\boldsymbol A_{[k]})$
  4. row swaps do not change the minors $\det(\boldsymbol A_{[k]})$, hence they can always be avoided
Explanation. Those operations do not change the leading minors; after $k-1$ steps the leading submatrix of order $k$ is triangular, hence $\det(\boldsymbol A_{[k]}) = u_{11}\cdots u_{kk}$ and the pivot $u_{kk} = \det(\boldsymbol A_{[k]})/\det(\boldsymbol A_{[k-1]})$ is nonzero. A swap, instead, may change the leading minors.
la-f-lu-10 · level facile · notes
Given the factorization $\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$ ($\boldsymbol L$ lower triangular with ones on the diagonal, $\boldsymbol U$ upper triangular nonsingular), how is $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ solved?
  1. compute $\boldsymbol L^{-1}$ and $\boldsymbol U^{-1}$ by Gauss–Jordan, then $\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol L^{-1}\boldsymbol U^{-1}\boldsymbol b$
  2. $\boldsymbol L\boldsymbol y = \boldsymbol b$ by forward substitution, then $\boldsymbol U\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol y$ by backward substitution
  3. $\boldsymbol U\boldsymbol y = \boldsymbol b$ by forward substitution, then $\boldsymbol L\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol y$ by backward substitution
  4. $\boldsymbol L\boldsymbol y = \boldsymbol b$ by backward substitution, then $\boldsymbol U\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol y$ by forward substitution
Explanation. $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol L(\boldsymbol U\boldsymbol x) = \boldsymbol b$: setting $\boldsymbol y = \boldsymbol U\boldsymbol x$, first solve the lower triangular system $\boldsymbol L\boldsymbol y = \boldsymbol b$ (forward), then the upper triangular system $\boldsymbol U\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol y$ (backward). The inverse of $\boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$ would be $\boldsymbol U^{-1}\boldsymbol L^{-1}$.
la-f-lu-11 · level facile · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$ with $\boldsymbol L = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ and $\boldsymbol U = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 1 \\ 0 & 4 \end{pmatrix}$. What is $\det(\boldsymbol A)$?
  1. $7$
  2. $24$
  3. $-12$
  4. $12$
Explanation. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = \det(\boldsymbol L)\det(\boldsymbol U) = 1\cdot(3\cdot 4) = 12$: $\boldsymbol L$ has ones on the diagonal and no swap changes the sign. $7$ is the trace of $\boldsymbol U$.
la-f-lu-12 · level facile · notes
The factorization $\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$ of $\boldsymbol A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 6 & 5 \end{pmatrix}$ is computed by Gaussian elimination without swaps ($\boldsymbol L$ lower triangular with ones on the diagonal). What are $\boldsymbol L$ and $\boldsymbol U$?
  1. $\boldsymbol L = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 3 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, $\boldsymbol U = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 0 & 5 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\boldsymbol L = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ \frac{1}{3} & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, $\boldsymbol U = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 0 & \frac{14}{3} \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\boldsymbol L = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 3 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, $\boldsymbol U = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\boldsymbol L = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ -3 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, $\boldsymbol U = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. The step $R_2 \leftarrow R_2 - 3R_1$ has multiplier $\ell_{21} = 6/2 = 3$, which goes in position $(2,1)$ of $\boldsymbol L$ with its sign, and gives $u_{22} = 5 - 3\cdot 1 = 2$. Check: $\boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U = \boldsymbol A$.
la-f-lu-k01 · level facile · notes
From $\boldsymbol P\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$ with unit-diagonal $\boldsymbol L$, $\boldsymbol U$ with diagonal $(2, -1, 3)$ and $\boldsymbol P$ obtained with one row swap, what is $\det(\boldsymbol A)$?
  1. $-6$
  2. $4$
  3. $-4$
  4. $6$
Explanation. $\det(\boldsymbol P) = -1$, $\det(\boldsymbol U) = -6$: $\det(\boldsymbol A) = \det(\boldsymbol U)/\det(\boldsymbol P) = 6$.
la-f-lu-k02 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the existence of the LU factorization (without permutations) is correct?
  1. It exists only for symmetric matrices
  2. It exists only if $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 1$
  3. It exists if no row swaps are needed
  4. It exists for every square matrix
Explanation. With a zero pivot a permutation is needed: $\boldsymbol P\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$.
la-f-lu-k03 · level ipotesi · notes
The matrix $\begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$ has no LU factorization without permutations. Which fact explains it?
  1. It is not symmetric
  2. It has negative entries
  3. The first pivot $a_{11}$ is zero
  4. The determinant is zero
Explanation. Rows must be swapped: $\boldsymbol P\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$ is used.

4.5. Eigenvalues and eigenvectors (32)

la-f-eig-01 · level facile · notes
A scalar $\lambda$ is an eigenvalue of $\boldsymbol Q \in \R^{n\times n}$ if:
  1. there exists $\boldsymbol v \in \R^n$ with $\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol v = \lambda\boldsymbol v$
  2. $\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol v = \lambda\boldsymbol v$ for every $\boldsymbol v \in \R^n$
  3. $\lambda$ equals $\det(\boldsymbol Q)$
  4. there exists $\boldsymbol v \neq \boldsymbol 0$ with $\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol v = \lambda\boldsymbol v$
Explanation. A nonzero vector is required: $\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol 0 = \lambda\boldsymbol 0$ holds for every $\lambda$. The condition is equivalent to $\det(\boldsymbol Q - \lambda\boldsymbol I) = 0$.
la-f-eig-02 · level facile · notes
What is the characteristic equation of $\boldsymbol Q \in \R^{n\times n}$?
  1. $\det(\boldsymbol Q - \lambda\boldsymbol I) = 0$
  2. $\det(\boldsymbol Q) - \lambda = 0$
  3. $\boldsymbol Q - \lambda\boldsymbol I = \boldsymbol 0$
  4. $\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol Q) - \lambda = 0$
Explanation. $\lambda$ is an eigenvalue if and only if $(\boldsymbol Q - \lambda\boldsymbol I)\boldsymbol v = \boldsymbol 0$ has nonzero solutions, i.e. if and only if $\det(\boldsymbol Q - \lambda\boldsymbol I) = 0$: a polynomial of degree $n$ in $\lambda$.
la-f-eig-03 · level facile · notes
What are the eigenvalues of $\boldsymbol U = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 & 3 \\ 0 & -1 & 4 \\ 0 & 0 & 5 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. $2,\ 4,\ 5$
  2. $-2,\ 1,\ -5$
  3. $6$ (the trace)
  4. $2,\ -1,\ 5$
Explanation. For a triangular matrix $\det(\boldsymbol U - \lambda\boldsymbol I) = (2-\lambda)(-1-\lambda)(5-\lambda)$: the eigenvalues are the diagonal entries.
la-f-eig-04 · level facile · notes
A matrix $\boldsymbol Q \in \R^{2\times 2}$ has eigenvalues $1$ and $3$. What are the trace $\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol Q)$ (sum of the diagonal entries) and $\det(\boldsymbol Q)$?
  1. they cannot be determined without knowing $\boldsymbol Q$
  2. $\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol Q) = 4$, $\det(\boldsymbol Q) = 3$
  3. $\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol Q) = 3$, $\det(\boldsymbol Q) = 4$
  4. $\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol Q) = 4$, $\det(\boldsymbol Q) = 4$
Explanation. The trace is the sum of the eigenvalues, the determinant their product: $1 + 3 = 4$ and $1\cdot 3 = 3$.
la-f-eig-05 · level teorema · notes
Let $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_n \in \C$ be the roots of the characteristic polynomial of $\boldsymbol Q \in \R^{n\times n}$, repeated according to multiplicity, and let $\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol Q)$ be the trace (sum of the diagonal entries). Which statement is true?
  1. $\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol Q) = \sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_i$, but only if all the roots are real and distinct
  2. $\det(\boldsymbol Q) = \prod_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_i$ and $\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol Q) = \sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_i$
  3. $\det(\boldsymbol Q) = \sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_i$ and $\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol Q) = \prod_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_i$
  4. $\det(\boldsymbol Q) = \prod_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_i$, but only if $\boldsymbol Q$ is triangular
Explanation. It holds for every square matrix, counting multiplicities and complex roots: for instance $\begin{pmatrix} 0 & -1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$ has roots $\pm i$, trace $0$ and determinant $1$.
la-f-eig-06 · level teorema · notes
Let $\boldsymbol Q$ be symmetric. Which statement is true?
  1. $\boldsymbol Q$ is positive definite if and only if $\det(\boldsymbol Q) > 0$
  2. $\boldsymbol Q$ is positive semidefinite if and only if $\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol Q) \ge 0$
  3. $\boldsymbol Q$ is positive semidefinite if and only if all its eigenvalues are $\ge 0$
  4. $\boldsymbol Q$ is positive semidefinite if and only if all its entries are $\ge 0$
Explanation. The sign of the quadratic form is determined by the signs of the eigenvalues. $\det(\boldsymbol Q) > 0$ also holds for $-\boldsymbol I_2$, which is negative definite.
la-f-eig-07 · level facile · notes
How is the symmetric matrix $\boldsymbol Q = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ classified according to the sign of the quadratic form $\boldsymbol x'\boldsymbol Q\,\boldsymbol x$?
  1. positive definite
  2. positive semidefinite
  3. negative definite
  4. indefinite
Explanation. $\Delta_1 = 1 > 0$ and $\Delta_2 = 1 - 4 = -3 < 0$; the eigenvalues are $3$ and $-1$, one positive and one negative.
la-f-eig-08 · level teorema · notes
Let $\boldsymbol Q \in \R^{n\times n}$ be symmetric and let $\Delta_k$ be its leading principal minor of order $k$ (determinant of the first $k$ rows and columns). Which statement of Sylvester's criterion is correct?
  1. $\boldsymbol Q$ is positive definite if and only if $\Delta_n = \det(\boldsymbol Q) > 0$
  2. $\boldsymbol Q$ is positive definite if and only if $\Delta_k > 0$ for every $k$
  3. $\boldsymbol Q$ is negative definite if and only if $\Delta_k < 0$ for every $k$
  4. $\boldsymbol Q$ is positive semidefinite if and only if $\Delta_k \ge 0$ for every $k$
Explanation. For negative definiteness the minors alternate in sign starting with $\Delta_1 < 0$; for semidefiniteness all principal minors must be checked, not only the leading ones.
la-f-eig-09 · level facile · notes
The leading principal minors (determinants of the first $k$ rows and columns) of $\boldsymbol Q = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & -1 \end{pmatrix}$ are $\Delta_1 = 0$ and $\Delta_2 = 0$. Is $\boldsymbol Q$ positive semidefinite?
  1. yes, because $\det(\boldsymbol Q) = 0 \ge 0$
  2. yes, and it is even positive definite
  3. no: the minor $q_{22} = -1$ is negative
  4. yes, because $\Delta_1 \ge 0$ and $\Delta_2 \ge 0$
Explanation. Leading minors are not enough for semidefiniteness: with $\boldsymbol x = (0, 1)'$ we get $\boldsymbol x'\boldsymbol Q\,\boldsymbol x = -1 < 0$. The eigenvalues are $0$ and $-1$: it is negative semidefinite.
la-f-eig-10 · level teorema · notes
Let $\boldsymbol v_1, \dots, \boldsymbol v_k$ be eigenvectors of $\boldsymbol Q$ associated with distinct eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_k$. Then:
  1. $\boldsymbol v_1, \dots, \boldsymbol v_k$ are linearly dependent if $k \ge 2$
  2. $\boldsymbol v_i'\boldsymbol v_j = 1$ for every $i \neq j$
  3. $\boldsymbol v_1, \dots, \boldsymbol v_k$ are linearly independent
  4. $\boldsymbol v_1, \dots, \boldsymbol v_k$ all have the same norm
Explanation. Eigenvectors of distinct eigenvalues are always linearly independent; with $n$ distinct real eigenvalues we get $n$ independent vectors of $\R^n$.
la-f-eig-11 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $\boldsymbol Q \in \R^{n\times n}$ be triangular. How is it proved that its eigenvalues (roots of $\det(\boldsymbol Q - \lambda\boldsymbol I) = 0$) are the diagonal entries $q_{11}, \dots, q_{nn}$?
  1. by Sylvester's criterion: the leading minors are $q_{11}$, $q_{11}q_{22}$, $\dots$
  2. $\boldsymbol Q - \lambda\boldsymbol I$ is triangular, hence $\det(\boldsymbol Q - \lambda\boldsymbol I) = \prod_i (q_{ii} - \lambda)$
  3. $\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol Q) = \sum_i \lambda_i$, hence each $q_{ii}$ is one of the eigenvalues $\lambda_i$
  4. $\det(\boldsymbol Q) = \prod_i \lambda_i$, hence each $q_{ii}$ is one of the eigenvalues $\lambda_i$
Explanation. The determinant of a triangular matrix is the product of its diagonal: the characteristic equation is $\prod_i (q_{ii} - \lambda) = 0$, with roots $q_{11}, \dots, q_{nn}$. Trace and determinant alone do not determine the eigenvalues.
la-f-eig-12 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $\boldsymbol Q$ be symmetric positive semidefinite, i.e. $\boldsymbol x'\boldsymbol Q\,\boldsymbol x \ge 0$ for every $\boldsymbol x$. Which argument proves that each of its eigenvalues $\lambda$ satisfies $\lambda \ge 0$?
  1. from $\operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol Q) = \sum_i \lambda_i \ge 0$ it follows that every term $\lambda_i$ is $\ge 0$
  2. from $q_{ii} = \boldsymbol e_i'\boldsymbol Q\,\boldsymbol e_i \ge 0$ it follows that the eigenvalues, i.e. the $q_{ii}$, are $\ge 0$
  3. from $\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol v = \lambda\boldsymbol v$, $\boldsymbol v \neq \boldsymbol 0$: $0 \le \boldsymbol v'\boldsymbol Q\,\boldsymbol v = \lambda\,\boldsymbol v'\boldsymbol v$ with $\boldsymbol v'\boldsymbol v > 0$
  4. from $\det(\boldsymbol Q) = \prod_i \lambda_i \ge 0$ it follows that every factor $\lambda_i$ is $\ge 0$
Explanation. Choose $\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol v$, an eigenvector of $\lambda$: $\lambda\,\boldsymbol v'\boldsymbol v \ge 0$ with $\boldsymbol v'\boldsymbol v > 0$, hence $\lambda \ge 0$. A non-negative product or sum does not make all factors or terms non-negative; the eigenvalues are the diagonal entries only for triangular matrices.
la-f-eig-13 · level facile · notes
If $\boldsymbol v$ is an eigenvector of $\boldsymbol Q$ associated with $\lambda$, what can be said of $3\boldsymbol v$?
  1. it is also an eigenvector associated with $\lambda$
  2. it is an eigenvector associated with $3\lambda$
  3. it is not an eigenvector, because eigenvectors are unique
  4. it is an eigenvector associated with $\lambda/3$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol Q(\alpha\boldsymbol v) = \alpha\lambda\boldsymbol v = \lambda(\alpha\boldsymbol v)$ for every $\alpha \neq 0$: eigenvectors are never unique.
la-f-eig-14 · level facile · notes
A principal minor of $\boldsymbol Q \in \R^{n\times n}$ is the determinant of the submatrix keeping the rows and columns with the same indices $i_1 < \dots < i_k$ ($k \ge 1$). How many principal minors does a matrix $\boldsymbol Q \in \R^{3\times 3}$ have?
  1. $3$
  2. $9$
  3. $6$
  4. $7$
Explanation. One for every nonempty set of indices in $\{1, 2, 3\}$: $3$ of order $1$ ($q_{11}, q_{22}, q_{33}$), $3$ of order $2$ and $1$ of order $3$, $2^3 - 1 = 7$ in total. The leading principal minors are only $3$.
la-f-eig-15 · level facile · notes
What can be said about the real eigenvalues of $\boldsymbol R = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & -1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$, i.e. the real roots of $\det(\boldsymbol R - \lambda\boldsymbol I) = 0$?
  1. they are $0$ and $0$: the diagonal entries
  2. they are $1$ and $-1$: the roots of $\lambda^2 - 1$
  3. there is only $1$, double: $\det(\boldsymbol R) = 1$
  4. there are none: $\lambda^2 + 1 = 0$ has roots $\pm i$
Explanation. $\det(\boldsymbol R - \lambda\boldsymbol I) = \lambda^2 + 1$ has no real roots; the complex roots $\pm i$ match trace $0$ and determinant $1$. $\boldsymbol R$ is not triangular.
la-f-eig-16 · level teorema · notes
To conclude that a symmetric matrix $\boldsymbol Q$ is positive semidefinite, it is enough to check that (principal minor: determinant of the submatrix with rows and columns of the same indices; leading: first $k$ rows and columns):
  1. $\det(\boldsymbol Q) \ge 0$ and the trace is $\ge 0$
  2. the diagonal entries are all $\ge 0$
  3. all principal minors are $\ge 0$
  4. the leading principal minors are $\ge 0$
Explanation. Semidefiniteness requires all principal minors: $\begin{pmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & -1 \end{pmatrix}$ has zero leading minors but is not positive semidefinite.
la-f-eig-17 · level facile · notes
What are the eigenvalues, repeated according to multiplicity, of $\boldsymbol Q = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$, i.e. the roots of $\det(\boldsymbol Q - \lambda\boldsymbol I) = 0$?
  1. $-1,\ 3,\ 3$
  2. $1,\ 3,\ 3$
  3. $2,\ 2,\ 3$
  4. $1,\ 2,\ 3$
Explanation. Expanding along the third row: $\det(\boldsymbol Q - \lambda\boldsymbol I) = (3 - \lambda)\big((2-\lambda)^2 - 1\big) = (3-\lambda)(\lambda - 1)(\lambda - 3)$. The matrix is not triangular: the diagonal entries $2, 2, 3$ are not the eigenvalues.
la-f-eig-18 · level facile · notes
The matrix $\boldsymbol Q = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$ has eigenvalues $1$, $3$, $3$. Which vector $\boldsymbol v \neq \boldsymbol 0$ satisfies $\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol v = 1\cdot\boldsymbol v$?
  1. $(1,\, 0,\, -1)'$
  2. $(1,\, -1,\, 0)'$
  3. $(1,\, 1,\, 0)'$
  4. $(0,\, 0,\, 1)'$
Explanation. $(\boldsymbol Q - \boldsymbol I)\boldsymbol v = \boldsymbol 0$ gives $v_1 + v_2 = 0$ and $2v_3 = 0$: $\boldsymbol v = (1,\, -1,\, 0)'$. $(1,\, 1,\, 0)'$ and $(0,\, 0,\, 1)'$ are eigenvectors of the eigenvalue $3$.
la-f-eigen-k01 · level facile · notes
$\lambda$ is an eigenvalue of $\boldsymbol A$, with eigenvector $\boldsymbol v \neq \boldsymbol 0$, if $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol v = \lambda\boldsymbol v$. For $\boldsymbol A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$, which pair is correct?
  1. $\boldsymbol v = (1, 0)'$, $\lambda = 2$
  2. $\boldsymbol v = (1, 1)'$, $\lambda = 2$
  3. $\boldsymbol v = (0, 0)'$, $\lambda = 0$
  4. $\boldsymbol v = (1, 1)'$, $\lambda = 3$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol A(1, 1)' = (3, 3)' = 3\,(1, 1)'$. $\boldsymbol A(1, 0)' = (2, 1)'$ is not a multiple of $(1, 0)'$; the zero vector is never an eigenvector.
la-f-eigen-k02 · level facile · notes
For $\boldsymbol A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 0 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $\boldsymbol v_1 = (1, 0)'$ is an eigenvector of $\lambda = 1$ and $\boldsymbol v_2 = (1, 1)'$ of $\lambda = 3$. What can be said about $\boldsymbol v_1, \boldsymbol v_2$?
  1. They have the same norm
  2. They form a basis of $\R^2$
  3. They are linearly dependent
  4. They are orthogonal
Explanation. Distinct eigenvalues give independent eigenvectors, and two independent vectors of $\R^2$ form a basis. They are not orthogonal: $\boldsymbol v_1'\boldsymbol v_2 = 1$.
la-f-eigen-k03 · level facile · notes
What is the trace of $\begin{pmatrix} 3 & 7 \\ -2 & 5 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. $8$
  2. $29$
  3. $13$
  4. $15$
Explanation. Sum of the diagonal entries: $3 + 5$.
la-f-eigen-k04 · level facile · notes
A $2 \times 2$ matrix has trace $7$ and determinant $10$. What are its eigenvalues?
  1. $1$ and $10$
  2. $7$ and $10$
  3. $2$ and $5$
  4. $3$ and $4$
Explanation. Sum $7$, product $10$.
la-f-eigen-k05 · level facile · notes
Eigenvectors associated with two distinct eigenvalues of a matrix are:
  1. linearly dependent
  2. linearly independent
  3. always orthogonal
  4. multiples of each other
Explanation. Independence always holds; orthogonality only for symmetric matrices.
la-f-eigen-k06 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that eigenvectors of distinct eigenvalues are independent, which assumption is essential?
  1. That the eigenvalues are pairwise distinct
  2. That the matrix is symmetric
  3. That the matrix is upper triangular
  4. That the eigenvectors all have norm $1$
Explanation. With equal eigenvalues the eigenvectors can be proportional.
la-f-eigen-k07 · level facile · notes
For a $3 \times 3$ matrix with eigenvalues $1$, $2$, $-3$, what are trace and determinant?
  1. Trace $6$, determinant $0$
  2. Trace $-6$, determinant $0$
  3. Trace $0$, determinant $6$
  4. Trace $0$, determinant $-6$
Explanation. Trace = sum, determinant = product of the eigenvalues.
la-f-eigen-k08 · level facile · notes
An eigenvalue $\lambda$ of a matrix $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{3\times 3}$ is known (a root of $\det(\boldsymbol A - \lambda\boldsymbol I) = 0$). How are the eigenvectors associated with $\lambda$ found?
  1. By solving $(\boldsymbol A - \lambda\boldsymbol I)\boldsymbol v = \boldsymbol 0$
  2. By computing $\det(\boldsymbol A)$
  3. By inverting $\boldsymbol A - \lambda\boldsymbol I$
  4. By taking the columns of $\boldsymbol A$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol A - \lambda\boldsymbol I$ is singular: the system has nonzero solutions.
la-f-eigen-k09 · level facile · notes
What are the eigenvalues of $\mathrm{diag}(5, -2, 0)$?
  1. $5$, $-2$, $0$
  2. $5$ and $-2$ only
  3. $3$
  4. $1$, $1$, $1$
Explanation. For diagonal (and triangular) matrices the eigenvalues are the diagonal entries, $0$ included.
la-f-eigen-k10 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the eigenvalues of a triangular matrix is correct?
  1. They are always positive numbers
  2. They are the entries of the main diagonal
  3. They are the entries of the last row of the matrix
  4. They all equal the trace
Explanation. $\det(\boldsymbol A - \lambda\boldsymbol I) = \prod_i (a_{ii} - \lambda)$.
la-f-eigen-k11 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that the eigenvalues of a triangular matrix are its diagonal entries, which fact is used?
  1. That the matrix is invertible
  2. Cramer's rule
  3. $\boldsymbol A - \lambda\boldsymbol I$ stays triangular
  4. That the matrix is symmetric
Explanation. $\det(\boldsymbol A - \lambda\boldsymbol I) = 0 \iff \lambda = a_{ii}$ for some $i$.
la-f-eigen-k12 · level teorema · notes
What does Sylvester's criterion state for a symmetric matrix $\boldsymbol Q$?
  1. $\boldsymbol Q$ is positive definite iff its trace and determinant are positive
  2. $\boldsymbol Q$ is positive definite iff its leading principal minors are all positive
  3. $\boldsymbol Q$ is positive definite iff all entries of $\boldsymbol Q$ are positive
  4. $\boldsymbol Q$ is positive definite iff the determinant $\det(\boldsymbol Q)$ is positive
Explanation. All leading principal minors $\Delta_1, \dots, \Delta_n$ are needed, not only the determinant; positive trace and determinant suffice only for $n = 2$, and positive entries never suffice.
la-f-eigen-k13 · level facile · notes
A matrix $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{3\times 3}$ has three distinct eigenvalues $1$, $2$, $5$, with eigenvectors $\boldsymbol v_1, \boldsymbol v_2, \boldsymbol v_3$. Which statement is true?
  1. $\boldsymbol v_1, \boldsymbol v_2, \boldsymbol v_3$ may be dependent
  2. $\boldsymbol v_1, \boldsymbol v_2, \boldsymbol v_3$ form a basis of $\R^3$
  3. $\boldsymbol v_1, \boldsymbol v_2, \boldsymbol v_3$ are always orthogonal
  4. $\boldsymbol v_1, \boldsymbol v_2, \boldsymbol v_3$ all have norm $1$
Explanation. Eigenvectors of distinct eigenvalues are linearly independent, and three independent vectors of $\R^3$ form a basis. Orthogonality is not guaranteed (only for symmetric matrices).
la-f-eigen-k14 · level facile · notes
Let $\boldsymbol v_1, \boldsymbol v_2$ be eigenvectors of $\boldsymbol A$ with distinct eigenvalues $\lambda_1 \neq \lambda_2$. Is the vector $\boldsymbol v_1 + \boldsymbol v_2$ an eigenvector of $\boldsymbol A$?
  1. Yes, with eigenvalue $\frac{\lambda_1 + \lambda_2}{2}$
  2. Yes, with eigenvalue $\lambda_1\lambda_2$
  3. No, never
  4. Yes, with eigenvalue $\lambda_1 + \lambda_2$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol A(\boldsymbol v_1 + \boldsymbol v_2) = \lambda_1\boldsymbol v_1 + \lambda_2\boldsymbol v_2$; if it were $\mu(\boldsymbol v_1 + \boldsymbol v_2)$, the independence of $\boldsymbol v_1, \boldsymbol v_2$ would give $\lambda_1 = \mu = \lambda_2$, a contradiction.

Part: Norms (77)

5. Norms (77)

la-f-norm-01 · level facile · notes
What is the Euclidean norm $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = \sqrt{\sum_j p_j^2}$ for $\boldsymbol p = (3,\, -4,\, 12)'$?
  1. $19$
  2. $12$
  3. $169$
  4. $13$
Explanation. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = \sqrt{9 + 16 + 144} = \sqrt{169} = 13$. $19$ is the $\ell_1$ norm, $12$ the $\ell_\infty$ norm, $169$ forgets the square root.
la-f-norm-02 · level facile · notes
What is the norm $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = \sum_j |p_j|$ for $\boldsymbol p = (3,\, -4,\, 12)'$?
  1. $13$
  2. $12$
  3. $19$
  4. $11$
Explanation. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = |3| + |-4| + |12| = 19$: the absolute values are added. $11$ forgets the absolute value.
la-f-norm-03 · level facile · notes
What is the norm $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty = \max_j |p_j|$ for $\boldsymbol p = (3,\, -14,\, 12)'$?
  1. $29$
  2. $3$
  3. $14$
  4. $12$
Explanation. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty = \max_j |p_j| = \max\{3, 14, 12\} = 14$; the maximum without absolute value would give $12$.
la-f-norm-04 · level teorema · notes
Which property holds for every norm $\|\cdot\|$ on $\R^n$, for all $\boldsymbol p, \boldsymbol w \in \R^n$ and $\lambda \in \R$?
  1. $\|\lambda\,\boldsymbol p\| = \lambda\,\|\boldsymbol p\|$
  2. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\| = \|\boldsymbol p\| + \|\boldsymbol w\|$
  3. $\|\boldsymbol p\| = 0$ for some $\boldsymbol p \neq \boldsymbol 0$
  4. $\|\lambda\,\boldsymbol p\| = |\lambda|\,\|\boldsymbol p\|$
Explanation. It is absolute homogeneity: the scalar comes out in absolute value (a norm is never negative). For the sum only the triangle inequality holds, and the norm vanishes only at $\boldsymbol 0$.
la-f-norm-05 · level teorema · notes
Let $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w = \sum_j p_j w_j$, $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = \sqrt{\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol p}$ and $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty = \max_j |p_j|$. Which inequality holds for every $\boldsymbol p, \boldsymbol w \in \R^n$ (Cauchy–Schwarz)?
  1. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_2 \ge \|\boldsymbol p\|_2 + \|\boldsymbol w\|_2$
  2. $|\boldsymbol p'\,\boldsymbol w| \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_2\,\|\boldsymbol w\|_2$
  3. $|\boldsymbol p'\,\boldsymbol w| \ge \|\boldsymbol p\|_2\,\|\boldsymbol w\|_2$
  4. $|\boldsymbol p'\,\boldsymbol w| \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty\,\|\boldsymbol w\|_\infty$
Explanation. It is the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. With $\boldsymbol p = \boldsymbol w = (1,1)'$ the third one gives $2 \le 1$, false; the last one reverses the triangle inequality.
la-f-norm-06 · level teorema · notes
Let $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = \sum_j |p_j|$, $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = \sqrt{\sum_j p_j^2}$ and $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty = \max_j |p_j|$. For every $\boldsymbol p \in \R^n$:
  1. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_2 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_1 \le n\,\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty$
  2. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_2 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty$
  3. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_1$
  4. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty \le n\,\|\boldsymbol p\|_2$
Explanation. For instance $\boldsymbol p = (3, -4, 12)'$: $12 \le 13 \le 19 \le 36$. Moreover $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 \le \sqrt n\,\|\boldsymbol p\|_2$.
la-f-norm-07 · level facile · notes
In $\R^2$, what shape is the set of points with $\|\boldsymbol x\|_1 = |x_1| + |x_2| \le 1$?
  1. a diamond centred at the origin
  2. the unit circle centred at the origin
  3. a square with sides parallel to the axes
  4. a rotated ellipse centred at the origin
Explanation. $|x_1| + |x_2| \le 1$ is a diamond with vertices $(\pm 1, 0)$ and $(0, \pm 1)$; the circle corresponds to $\ell_2$, the axis-aligned square to $\ell_\infty$.
la-f-norm-08 · level facile · notes
To define the generalized $\ell_2$ norm $\|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q} = \sqrt{\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\,\boldsymbol p}$, the matrix $\boldsymbol Q$ must be:
  1. invertible (nonzero determinant)
  2. symmetric positive definite
  3. symmetric, of any sign
  4. positive semidefinite
Explanation. We need $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\,\boldsymbol p \ge 0$ for the square root, and $= 0$ only for $\boldsymbol p = \boldsymbol 0$ for definiteness: hence positive definite. With $\boldsymbol Q = \boldsymbol I$ we get back $\|\cdot\|_2$.
la-f-norm-09 · level facile · notes
Let $\|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q} = \sqrt{\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\,\boldsymbol p}$ be the generalized $\ell_2$ norm, with $\boldsymbol Q = \begin{pmatrix} 4 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$. What is $\|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q}$ for $\boldsymbol p = (1,\, 2)'$?
  1. $\sqrt 5$
  2. $8$
  3. $\sqrt 6$
  4. $2\sqrt 2$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\,\boldsymbol p = 4\cdot 1^2 + 1\cdot 2^2 = 8$, hence $\|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q} = \sqrt 8 = 2\sqrt 2$. $\sqrt 5$ is $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2$, $8$ forgets the square root.
la-f-norm-10 · level ipotesi · notes
We want to prove the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality $|\boldsymbol p'\,\boldsymbol w| \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_2\,\|\boldsymbol w\|_2$ for every $\boldsymbol p, \boldsymbol w \in \R^n$. Which fact is the proof based on?
  1. the inequality $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_2$, applied both to $\boldsymbol p$ and to $\boldsymbol w$
  2. $\boldsymbol u = \alpha\boldsymbol p + \beta\boldsymbol w$ has $\boldsymbol u'\boldsymbol u \ge 0$ for every $\alpha, \beta \in \R$, with well-chosen $\alpha, \beta$
  3. the triangle inequality $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_2 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_2 + \|\boldsymbol w\|_2$, squared
  4. the inequality $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_1$, applied both to $\boldsymbol p$ and to $\boldsymbol w$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol u'\boldsymbol u = \alpha^2\,\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol p + 2\alpha\beta\,\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w + \beta^2\,\boldsymbol w'\boldsymbol w \ge 0$; with $\alpha = \boldsymbol w'\boldsymbol w$ and $\beta = -\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w$ the claim follows. The triangle inequality of the $\ell_2$ norm is in turn proved with Cauchy–Schwarz.
la-f-norm-11 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $\boldsymbol p \in \R^n$. Which step proves the inequality $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 \le \sqrt n\,\|\boldsymbol p\|_2$?
  1. the inequality $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_2$, multiplied by $\sqrt n$
  2. the triangle inequality of the $\ell_1$ norm applied to $\boldsymbol p = \sum_j p_j\boldsymbol e_j$
  3. Cauchy–Schwarz applied to $\boldsymbol 1 = (1, \dots, 1)'$ and to the vector $(|p_1|, \dots, |p_n|)'$
  4. Cauchy–Schwarz applied to $\boldsymbol 1 = (1, \dots, 1)'$ and to $\boldsymbol p$, without absolute values
Explanation. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = \sum_j 1\cdot|p_j| \le \|\boldsymbol 1\|_2\,\sqrt{\sum_j p_j^2} = \sqrt n\,\|\boldsymbol p\|_2$. Without absolute values only $|\sum_j p_j|$ is bounded; with $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 \le n\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty$ one would get the factor $n$.
la-f-norm-12 · level ipotesi · notes
The generalized Cauchy–Schwarz inequality is $|\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\,\boldsymbol w| \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q}\|\boldsymbol w\|_{\boldsymbol Q}$, with $\|\boldsymbol x\|_{\boldsymbol Q} = \sqrt{\boldsymbol x'\boldsymbol Q\,\boldsymbol x}$. Which assumptions on $\boldsymbol Q \in \R^{n\times n}$ does the proof use?
  1. $\boldsymbol Q$ symmetric and positive definite
  2. $\boldsymbol Q$ diagonal with nonzero diagonal entries
  3. $\boldsymbol Q$ invertible, with eigenvalues of any sign
  4. $\boldsymbol Q$ positive semidefinite, possibly not symmetric
Explanation. Symmetry gives $\boldsymbol w'\boldsymbol Q\,\boldsymbol p = \boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\,\boldsymbol w$; positive definiteness gives $\boldsymbol u'\boldsymbol Q\,\boldsymbol u \ge 0$ for every $\boldsymbol u$ and $\boldsymbol w'\boldsymbol Q\,\boldsymbol w > 0$ for $\boldsymbol w \neq \boldsymbol 0$, and makes $\|\cdot\|_{\boldsymbol Q}$ a norm.
la-f-norm-13 · level facile · notes
What is the Euclidean distance $\|\boldsymbol p - \boldsymbol w\|_2$ between $\boldsymbol p = (1,\, 4)'$ and $\boldsymbol w = (4,\, 1)'$?
  1. $3\sqrt 2$
  2. $6$
  3. $3$
  4. $18$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol p - \boldsymbol w = (-3, 3)'$ and $\sqrt{9 + 9} = \sqrt{18} = 3\sqrt 2$. $6$ is the $\ell_1$ distance, $3$ the $\ell_\infty$ distance.
la-f-norm-14 · level ipotesi · notes
How is the reverse triangle inequality $\big|\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 - \|\boldsymbol w\|_2\big| \le \|\boldsymbol p - \boldsymbol w\|_2$ obtained, for every $\boldsymbol p, \boldsymbol w \in \R^n$?
  1. from the triangle inequality applied only to $\boldsymbol p = (\boldsymbol p - \boldsymbol w) + \boldsymbol w$
  2. from Cauchy–Schwarz applied to the two vectors $\boldsymbol p - \boldsymbol w$ and $\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w$
  3. from homogeneity $\|\lambda\boldsymbol p\|_2 = |\lambda|\,\|\boldsymbol p\|_2$ with $\lambda = -1$
  4. from the triangle inequality applied to $\boldsymbol p = (\boldsymbol p - \boldsymbol w) + \boldsymbol w$ and to $\boldsymbol w = (\boldsymbol w - \boldsymbol p) + \boldsymbol p$
Explanation. The first expression gives $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 - \|\boldsymbol w\|_2 \le \|\boldsymbol p - \boldsymbol w\|_2$, the second $\|\boldsymbol w\|_2 - \|\boldsymbol p\|_2 \le \|\boldsymbol w - \boldsymbol p\|_2 = \|\boldsymbol p - \boldsymbol w\|_2$: together they give the absolute value. Only one of the two is not enough.
la-f-norm-15 · level facile · notes
Let $\|\boldsymbol x\|_{\boldsymbol Q} = \sqrt{\boldsymbol x'\boldsymbol Q\,\boldsymbol x}$ with $\boldsymbol Q = \begin{pmatrix} 4 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$. The set $\{\boldsymbol x \in \R^2 : \|\boldsymbol x\|_{\boldsymbol Q} \le 1\}$ is an ellipse with axes along the coordinate axes. What are the lengths of the semi-axes?
  1. $\frac{1}{4}$ and $1$
  2. $\frac{1}{2}$ and $1$
  3. $4$ and $1$
  4. $2$ and $1$
Explanation. The semi-axes have length $1/\sqrt{\lambda_i}$, with $\lambda_i$ the eigenvalues of $\boldsymbol Q$: $1/\sqrt 4 = \frac{1}{2}$ and $1/\sqrt 1 = 1$. Indeed $4x_1^2 + x_2^2 \le 1$.
la-f-norm-16 · level teorema · notes
A function $\|\cdot\|: \R^n \rightarrow \R$ is a norm if, for every $\boldsymbol p, \boldsymbol w \in \R^n$ and $\lambda \in \R$, $\|\boldsymbol p\| \ge 0$ with $\|\boldsymbol p\| = 0 \Leftrightarrow \boldsymbol p = \boldsymbol 0$, $\|\lambda\boldsymbol p\| = |\lambda|\,\|\boldsymbol p\|$ and moreover:
  1. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\| \ge \|\boldsymbol p\| + \|\boldsymbol w\|$
  2. $|\boldsymbol p'\,\boldsymbol w| \le \|\boldsymbol p\|\,\|\boldsymbol w\|$
  3. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\| \le \|\boldsymbol p\| + \|\boldsymbol w\|$
  4. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\| = \|\boldsymbol p\| + \|\boldsymbol w\|$
Explanation. The third axiom is the triangle inequality. Equality holds only in special cases (for instance $\boldsymbol w = \boldsymbol p$); Cauchy–Schwarz is a property of the $\ell_2$ norm, not an axiom.
la-f-norm-17 · level facile · notes
The $\ell_\infty$ norm is $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty = \max_j |p_j|$. For $\boldsymbol p = (1,\, -2)'$ and $\boldsymbol w = (3,\, 2)'$, what are $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_\infty$ and $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty + \|\boldsymbol w\|_\infty$?
  1. $4$ and $5$
  2. $4$ and $4$
  3. $5$ and $5$
  4. $3$ and $5$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w = (4,\, 0)'$, hence $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_\infty = 4$, while $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty + \|\boldsymbol w\|_\infty = 2 + 3 = 5$: here the triangle inequality is strict.
la-f-norms-k01 · level facile · notes
What are the three properties defining a norm $\|\cdot\|$ on $\R^n$?
  1. Linearity, symmetry, positivity
  2. Additivity, continuity, monotonicity
  3. Commutativity, associativity, distributivity
  4. Positivity, homogeneity, triangle inequality
Explanation. $\|\boldsymbol p\| \ge 0$ with $= 0$ only for $\boldsymbol 0$; $\|\alpha\boldsymbol p\| = |\alpha|\,\|\boldsymbol p\|$; $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\| \le \|\boldsymbol p\| + \|\boldsymbol w\|$.
la-f-norms-k02 · level facile · notes
Is a norm linear, i.e. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\| = \|\boldsymbol p\| + \|\boldsymbol w\|$ for all $\boldsymbol p, \boldsymbol w$?
  1. No: only $\le$ holds
  2. Yes, for every norm
  3. Yes, but only for the $\ell_1$ norm
  4. Yes, but only in $\R^2$
Explanation. For instance $\|(1,0)' + (-1,0)'\|_1 = 0 < 2$.
la-f-norms-k03 · level facile · notes
The $\ell_1$ norm of $\boldsymbol p \in \R^2$ is $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = |p_1| + |p_2|$. How many vectors $\boldsymbol p \in \R^2$ with integer components have $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = 1$?
  1. $2$
  2. $1$
  3. $8$
  4. $4$
Explanation. One component must be $\pm 1$ and the other $0$: $(1, 0)'$, $(-1, 0)'$, $(0, 1)'$, $(0, -1)'$, the vertices of the diamond $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = 1$. $2$ forgets the vectors with negative components.
la-f-norms-k04 · level facile · notes
Why is the $\ell_1$ norm also called the Manhattan norm?
  1. Because it is the straight-line distance
  2. Because it takes the maximum component
  3. It adds the moves along the axes
  4. Because it is the most used in New York
