Explanation. The displacements along each axis are added.
la-f-norms-k05 · level facile · notes
For the norm $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = \sum_j |p_j|$, when is $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = 0$?
  1. Only for $\boldsymbol p = \boldsymbol 0$
  2. For every $\boldsymbol p$ whose components sum to zero
  3. Never
  4. For vectors with negative components
Explanation. A sum of absolute values is zero only if all are zero.
la-f-norms-k06 · level facile · notes
For $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = \sum_j |p_j|$, what is $\|-3\,\boldsymbol p\|_1$ if $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = 4$?
  1. $12$
  2. $-12$
  3. $4$
  4. $36$
Explanation. Homogeneity: $|{-3}| \cdot 4$.
la-f-norms-k07 · level facile · notes
For $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = \sum_j |p_j|$ with $\boldsymbol p = (1, -2)'$ and $\boldsymbol w = (3, 2)'$, which relation holds?
  1. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_1 = 8 = \|\boldsymbol p\|_1 + \|\boldsymbol w\|_1$
  2. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_1 = 0 \le 8 = \|\boldsymbol p\|_1 + \|\boldsymbol w\|_1$
  3. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_1 = 4 \le 8 = \|\boldsymbol p\|_1 + \|\boldsymbol w\|_1$
  4. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_1 = 4 \le 6 = \|\boldsymbol p\|_1 + \|\boldsymbol w\|_1$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w = (4, 0)'$, so $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_1 = 4$, while $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = 3$ and $\|\boldsymbol w\|_1 = 5$: $4 \le 8$, with strict inequality.
la-f-norms-k08 · level ipotesi · notes
The triangle inequality for the $\ell_1$ norm is proved componentwise. Which fact is used?
  1. $|p_j + w_j| \le |p_j| + |w_j|$ for every $j$
  2. The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality
  3. That $\boldsymbol p$ and $\boldsymbol w$ are orthogonal
  4. That the components are positive
Explanation. The triangle inequality for reals is summed over all components.
la-f-norms-k09 · level facile · notes
The Euclidean norm of $\boldsymbol p \in \R^2$ is $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = \sqrt{p_1^2 + p_2^2}$. How many vectors $\boldsymbol p \in \R^2$ with integer components have $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = 5$?
  1. $4$
  2. $8$
  3. $6$
  4. $12$
Explanation. $p_1^2 + p_2^2 = 25$ with integers: $(\pm 5, 0)$, $(0, \pm 5)$ (there are $4$) and $(\pm 3, \pm 4)$, $(\pm 4, \pm 3)$ (there are $8$): $12$ in all. $4$ considers only the points on the axes.
la-f-norms-k10 · level facile · notes
How is the Euclidean norm expressed through the dot product?
  1. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = \sqrt{\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol p}$
  2. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = \boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol p$
  3. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = \boldsymbol p\,\boldsymbol p'$
  4. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = \sqrt{\boldsymbol p\,\boldsymbol p'}$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol p = \sum_j p_j^2$ is a scalar; $\boldsymbol p\,\boldsymbol p'$ is a matrix.
la-f-norms-k11 · level facile · notes
For $\boldsymbol p = (1, 2)'$ and $\boldsymbol w = (3, 1)'$, which check of Cauchy–Schwarz $|\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w| \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_2\|\boldsymbol w\|_2$ is correct?
  1. $5 \le \sqrt{5}\sqrt{10} = \sqrt{50}$
  2. $5 \le \sqrt{5}\sqrt{10} = 5$
  3. $7 \le \sqrt{5}\sqrt{10} = \sqrt{50}$
  4. $5 \le \sqrt{3}\sqrt{4} = \sqrt{12}$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w = 3 + 2 = 5$, $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = \sqrt 5$, $\|\boldsymbol w\|_2 = \sqrt{10}$ and $\sqrt{50} \approx 7.07 \ge 5$. $\sqrt5\sqrt{10}$ is not $5$; $7$ is not the scalar product; $\sqrt 3$ and $\sqrt 4$ are not the norms.
la-f-norms-k12 · level facile · notes
When does equality hold in the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality $|\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w| \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_2\|\boldsymbol w\|_2$?
  1. Never
  2. When they have the same norm
  3. When they are dependent
  4. When they are orthogonal
Explanation. Parallel vectors (or one of them zero).
la-f-norms-k13 · level teorema · notes
What does the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality state in $\R^n$?
  1. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_2 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_2 + \|\boldsymbol w\|_2$
  2. $|\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w| \ge \|\boldsymbol p\|_2\,\|\boldsymbol w\|_2$
  3. $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w = \|\boldsymbol p\|_2\,\|\boldsymbol w\|_2$
  4. $|\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w| \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_2\,\|\boldsymbol w\|_2$
Explanation. The second is the triangle inequality, which follows from it.
la-f-norms-k14 · level ipotesi · notes
A proof of Cauchy–Schwarz studies $f(t) = \|\boldsymbol p + t\boldsymbol w\|_2^2 \ge 0$, a quadratic polynomial in $t$. What is then used?
  1. The discriminant of $f$ is $\le 0$
  2. $f$ has two distinct real roots
  3. $f$ is linear
  4. $\boldsymbol p$ and $\boldsymbol w$ are orthogonal
Explanation. $f(t) = \|\boldsymbol w\|^2 t^2 + 2\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w\,t + \|\boldsymbol p\|^2 \ge 0$ for every $t$.
la-f-norms-k15 · level facile · notes
For $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = \sqrt{\sum_j p_j^2}$, which statement is correct?
  1. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = 0$ for every unit vector
  2. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 > 0$ also for $\boldsymbol p = \boldsymbol 0$
  3. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 \ge 0$, with equality only for $\boldsymbol p = \boldsymbol 0$
  4. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2$ can be negative for some $\boldsymbol p$
Explanation. Square root of a sum of squares.
la-f-norms-k16 · level facile · notes
If $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = 5$, what is $\|\frac{1}{5}\boldsymbol p\|_2$?
  1. $25$
  2. $1$
  3. $5$
  4. $\frac{1}{25}$
Explanation. Homogeneity: dividing by the norm gives a unit vector.
la-f-norms-k17 · level facile · notes
For the Euclidean norm, which inequality always holds?
  1. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_2 \ge \|\boldsymbol p\|_2 + \|\boldsymbol w\|_2$
  2. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_2^2 = \|\boldsymbol p\|_2^2 + \|\boldsymbol w\|_2^2$
  3. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 \le \|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_2$
  4. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_2 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_2 + \|\boldsymbol w\|_2$
Explanation. The third holds only for orthogonal vectors (Pythagoras).
la-f-norms-k18 · level ipotesi · notes
The triangle inequality for the $\ell_2$ norm is obtained by expanding $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_2^2$. Which result is needed?
  1. That $\boldsymbol p$ and $\boldsymbol w$ are positive
  2. Sylvester's criterion
  3. The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality
  4. The triangle inequality for reals only
Explanation. $\|\boldsymbol p\|^2 + 2\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w + \|\boldsymbol w\|^2 \le (\|\boldsymbol p\| + \|\boldsymbol w\|)^2$ uses $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w \le \|\boldsymbol p\|\|\boldsymbol w\|$.
la-f-norms-k19 · level facile · notes
For $\boldsymbol p = (3, 0)'$ and $\boldsymbol w = (0, 4)'$, what is $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_2$?
  1. $5$
  2. $7$
  3. $25$
  4. $1$
Explanation. $\sqrt{9 + 16} = 5 < 3 + 4$.
la-f-norms-k20 · level teorema · notes
Which reverse triangle inequality holds for the Euclidean norm?
  1. $\big|\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 - \|\boldsymbol w\|_2\big| \le \|\boldsymbol p - \boldsymbol w\|_2$
  2. $\|\boldsymbol p - \boldsymbol w\|_2 \le \big|\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 - \|\boldsymbol w\|_2\big|$
  3. $\|\boldsymbol p - \boldsymbol w\|_2 = \|\boldsymbol p\|_2 - \|\boldsymbol w\|_2$
  4. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 \le \|\boldsymbol w\|_2$
Explanation. It follows from the triangle inequality applied twice.
la-f-norms-k21 · level facile · notes
With the generalized $\ell_2$ norm $\|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q} = \sqrt{\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol p}$ and $\boldsymbol Q = \mathrm{diag}(4, 9)$, what is $\|(1, 1)'\|_{\boldsymbol Q}$?
  1. $5$
  2. $13$
  3. $\sqrt{2}$
  4. $\sqrt{13}$
Explanation. $\sqrt{4 + 9}$.
la-f-norms-k22 · level ipotesi · notes
For $\|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q} = \sqrt{\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol p}$ to be a norm, which assumption on $\boldsymbol Q$ is needed?
  1. $\boldsymbol Q$ symmetric positive definite
  2. $\boldsymbol Q$ with all entries positive
  3. any diagonal matrix $\boldsymbol Q$
  4. any invertible matrix $\boldsymbol Q$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol p > 0$ for $\boldsymbol p \neq \boldsymbol 0$ is needed.
la-f-norms-k23 · level facile · notes
What does the generalized Cauchy–Schwarz inequality state, for symmetric positive definite $\boldsymbol Q$?
  1. $|\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol w| \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q}\,\|\boldsymbol w\|_{\boldsymbol Q}$
  2. $|\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w| \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q}\,\|\boldsymbol w\|_{\boldsymbol Q}$
  3. $|\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol w| \ge \|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q}\,\|\boldsymbol w\|_{\boldsymbol Q}$
  4. $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol w = 0$
Explanation. With $\boldsymbol Q = \boldsymbol I$ we get Cauchy–Schwarz.
la-f-norms-k24 · level facile · notes
With $\boldsymbol Q = \mathrm{diag}(1, 4)$, $\boldsymbol p = (1, 1)'$, $\boldsymbol w = (2, 1)'$, what is $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol w$?
  1. $5$
  2. $6$
  3. $3$
  4. $9$
Explanation. $1 \cdot 1 \cdot 2 + 4 \cdot 1 \cdot 1 = 6$.
la-f-norms-k25 · level facile · notes
For symmetric positive definite $\boldsymbol Q$, when is $\|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q} = 0$?
  1. For every $\boldsymbol p$ in the kernel of $\boldsymbol Q$, even nonzero
  2. Never
  3. For $\boldsymbol p$ with negative components
  4. Only for $\boldsymbol p = \boldsymbol 0$
Explanation. Positive definite: $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol p > 0$ if $\boldsymbol p \neq \boldsymbol 0$.
la-f-norms-k26 · level facile · notes
If $\|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q} = 3$, what is $\|2\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q}$?
  1. $3$
  2. $6$
  3. $12$
  4. $9$
Explanation. $\sqrt{(2\boldsymbol p)'\boldsymbol Q(2\boldsymbol p)} = 2\sqrt{\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol p}$.
la-f-norms-k27 · level facile · notes
The triangle inequality $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_{\boldsymbol Q} \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q} + \|\boldsymbol w\|_{\boldsymbol Q}$ is proved with:
  1. Sylvester's criterion on $\boldsymbol Q$
  2. Cramer's rule
  3. the LU factorization of $\boldsymbol Q$
  4. Generalized Cauchy–Schwarz
Explanation. As for the Euclidean norm, with $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol w$ instead of $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w$.
la-f-norms-k28 · level facile · notes
With $\boldsymbol Q = \boldsymbol I$, the norm $\|\cdot\|_{\boldsymbol Q}$ coincides with:
  1. the Euclidean norm $\|\cdot\|_2$
  2. the norm $\|\cdot\|_1$
  3. the norm $\|\cdot\|_\infty$
  4. the absolute value of the sum of components
Explanation. $\sqrt{\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol I\boldsymbol p} = \sqrt{\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol p}$.
la-f-norms-k29 · level facile · notes
The $\ell_\infty$ norm is $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty = \max_j |p_j|$. Which vector has the largest $\ell_\infty$ norm?
  1. $(0,\, 0,\, -5)'$
  2. $(3,\, -3,\, 3)'$
  3. $(4,\, 4,\, 4)'$
  4. $(1,\, -2,\, 4)'$
Explanation. The $\ell_\infty$ norms are $5$, $3$, $4$ and $4$. $(4, 4, 4)'$ has the largest sum (and $\ell_1$ norm), but only the component of largest absolute value counts.
la-f-norms-k30 · level facile · notes
Which vectors of $\R^2$ have $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty \le 1$?
  1. Those of the diamond $|p_1| + |p_2| \le 1$
  2. Those with $p_1 + p_2 \le 1$
  3. Those of the square $[-1, 1] \times [-1, 1]$
  4. Those of the unit disc
Explanation. Each component at most $1$ in absolute value.
la-f-norms-k31 · level facile · notes
For $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty = \max_j |p_j|$, which statement is correct?
  1. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty = 0$ if one component is zero
  2. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty \ge \|\boldsymbol p\|_1$
  3. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty = 0$ only for $\boldsymbol p = \boldsymbol 0$
  4. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty$ can be negative
Explanation. The largest absolute value is zero only if all are.
la-f-norms-k32 · level facile · notes
If $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty = 4$, what is $\|-\frac12\boldsymbol p\|_\infty$?
  1. $2$
  2. $-2$
  3. $8$
  4. $4$
Explanation. Homogeneity: $|{-\frac12}| \cdot 4$.
la-f-norms-k33 · level facile · notes
For $\boldsymbol p = (1, -3)'$ and $\boldsymbol w = (2, 3)'$, which check of the triangle inequality for $\|\cdot\|_\infty$ is correct?
  1. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_\infty = 3 \le 3$
  2. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_\infty = 3 \le 6$
  3. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_\infty = 6 \le 6$
  4. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_\infty = 0 \le 6$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w = (3, 0)'$; $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty + \|\boldsymbol w\|_\infty = 3 + 3$.
la-f-norms-k34 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_\infty \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty + \|\boldsymbol w\|_\infty$, what is used?
  1. That $\boldsymbol p$ and $\boldsymbol w$ have the same maximum
  2. That the components are positive
  3. $|p_j + w_j| \le |p_j| + |w_j|$ for every $j$
  4. Cauchy–Schwarz
Explanation. Bound each component and then take the maximum.
la-f-norms-k35 · level facile · notes
For $\boldsymbol p = (3, -4)'$, in which order are $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty$, $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2$, $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1$?
  1. $5 \le 4 \le 7$
  2. $4 \le 7 \le 5$
  3. $4 \le 5 \le 7$
  4. $7 \le 5 \le 4$
Explanation. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_2 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_1$ always holds.
la-f-norms-k36 · level teorema · notes
Which chain of inequalities between norms holds for every $\boldsymbol p \in \R^n$?
  1. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = \|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = \|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty$
  2. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_2 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_1 \le n\,\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty$
  3. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_2 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty$
  4. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_1$
Explanation. The maximum does not exceed the root of the squares, which does not exceed the sum.
la-f-norms-k37 · level facile · notes
For which vector of $\R^3$ do the three norms $\|\cdot\|_1$, $\|\cdot\|_2$, $\|\cdot\|_\infty$ coincide?
  1. $(0, -5, 0)'$
  2. $(1, 1, 1)'$
  3. $(3, 4, 0)'$
  4. $(1, -1, 0)'$
Explanation. With a single nonzero component the three norms equal $|p_j|$.
la-f-norms-k38 · level facile · notes
For $\boldsymbol p = (1, 1, 1, 1)' \in \R^4$, what are $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1$, $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2$, $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty$?
  1. $1$, $2$, $4$
  2. $4$, $\sqrt{2}$, $1$
  3. $4$, $2$, $1$
  4. $4$, $4$, $1$
Explanation. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = \sqrt{4} = 2$: here $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = n\,\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty$.
la-f-norms-k39 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the Euclidean norm is correct?
  1. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2^2 = \boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol p$
  2. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = \sum_j p_j$
  3. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2$ depends on the order of components
  4. $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_2 = \|\boldsymbol p\|_2 + \|\boldsymbol w\|_2$ always
Explanation. A sum of squares: order does not matter.
la-f-norms-k40 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the three norms $\ell_1$, $\ell_2$, $\ell_\infty$ is correct?
  1. All three are norms
  2. Only the $\ell_2$ norm satisfies the triangle inequality
  3. The $\ell_\infty$ norm is not homogeneous
  4. The $\ell_1$ norm can vanish on nonzero vectors
Explanation. All three are norms, with different values in general.
la-f-norms-k41 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_1$, which fact suffices?
  1. Cauchy–Schwarz with $\boldsymbol w = \boldsymbol p$
  2. That $n = 2$, as in the plane
  3. That the components $p_j$ are integers
  4. $\big(\sum_j |p_j|\big)^2 = \sum_j p_j^2 + (\text{cross terms} \ge 0)$
Explanation. Squaring the sum of absolute values gives the squares plus nonnegative terms.
la-f-norms-k42 · level ipotesi · notes
Homogeneity $\|\alpha\boldsymbol p\|_1 = |\alpha|\,\|\boldsymbol p\|_1$ is proved with which property of reals?
  1. $|\alpha\,p_j| = |\alpha|\,|p_j|$
  2. $|\alpha + p_j| \le |\alpha| + |p_j|$
  3. $\alpha\,p_j \ge 0$ for every $j$
  4. $\alpha = 1$ or $\alpha = -1$
Explanation. $|\alpha|$ is factored out of the sum.
la-f-norms-k43 · level ipotesi · notes
The generalized Cauchy–Schwarz inequality $|\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol w| \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q}\|\boldsymbol w\|_{\boldsymbol Q}$ requires which assumption on $\boldsymbol Q$?
  1. None: it holds for every $\boldsymbol Q$
  2. $\boldsymbol Q$ symmetric positive definite
  3. $\boldsymbol Q$ upper triangular
  4. $\boldsymbol Q$ with determinant $1$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol w$ must be an inner product.
la-f-norms-k44 · level teorema · notes
For symmetric positive definite $\boldsymbol Q$, which statement is correct?
  1. $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol p$ can be negative
  2. $\|\cdot\|_{\boldsymbol Q}$ fails the triangle inequality
  3. $\|\cdot\|_{\boldsymbol Q} = \|\cdot\|_1$
  4. $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol w$ is an inner product
Explanation. Symmetry, bilinearity and positivity: it is an inner product.
la-f-norms-k45 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the norm $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty = \max_j |p_j|$ is correct?
  1. It can be negative
  2. It is the largest $|p_j|$ and a norm
  3. It is the sum of absolute values
  4. It fails the triangle inequality
Explanation. It is also called the maximum or Chebyshev norm.
la-f-norms-k46 · level facile · notes
A norm on $\R^2$ must be $\ge 0$, zero only at the zero vector, homogeneous and subadditive. Which function of $\boldsymbol p = (p_1, p_2)'$ is not a norm?
  1. $f(\boldsymbol p) = |p_1|$
  2. $f(\boldsymbol p) = |p_1| + |p_2|$
  3. $f(\boldsymbol p) = \max\{|p_1|, |p_2|\}$
  4. $f(\boldsymbol p) = \sqrt{p_1^2 + p_2^2}$
Explanation. $|p_1|$ is $0$ also for $\boldsymbol p = (0, 1)' \neq \boldsymbol 0$: it violates definiteness. The others are the $\ell_1$, $\ell_\infty$ and $\ell_2$ norms.
la-f-norms-k47 · level facile · notes
Let $\|\cdot\|$ be a norm on $\R^n$ (in particular $\|\lambda\,\boldsymbol p\| = |\lambda|\,\|\boldsymbol p\|$ for every $\lambda \in \R$). What is $\|-\boldsymbol p\|$?
  1. $-\|\boldsymbol p\|$
  2. $0$
  3. $2\,\|\boldsymbol p\|$
  4. $\|\boldsymbol p\|$
Explanation. With $\lambda = -1$: $\|-\boldsymbol p\| = |-1|\,\|\boldsymbol p\| = \|\boldsymbol p\|$. Reversing the direction does not change the length; $-\|\boldsymbol p\|$ would be negative.
la-f-norms-k48 · level facile · notes
The $\ell_1$ norm is $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = \sum_j |p_j|$. What is $\|\boldsymbol p - \boldsymbol w\|_1$ for $\boldsymbol p = (2, -1, 4)'$ and $\boldsymbol w = (5, 1, 4)'$?
  1. $3$
  2. $5$
  3. $-5$
  4. $17$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol p - \boldsymbol w = (-3, -2, 0)'$ and $|-3| + |-2| + 0 = 5$. $-5$ forgets the absolute values; $17 = 7 + 10$ adds the norms of the two vectors; $3 = |7 - 10|$ subtracts the norms.
la-f-norms-k49 · level facile · notes
The Euclidean norm is $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = \sqrt{\sum_j p_j^2}$. For which $t > 0$ does the vector $(t, t)' \in \R^2$ have $\|(t, t)'\|_2 = 1$?
  1. $t = \sqrt 2$
  2. $t = \frac{1}{\sqrt 2}$
  3. $t = \frac12$
  4. $t = 1$
Explanation. $\sqrt{t^2 + t^2} = \sqrt 2\, t = 1$ gives $t = \frac{1}{\sqrt 2}$. With $t = \frac12$ the $\ell_1$ norm equals $1$, not the $\ell_2$ one.
la-f-norms-k50 · level facile · notes
With the Euclidean norm $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = \sqrt{\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol p}$, what does $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_2^2$ equal for $\boldsymbol p, \boldsymbol w \in \R^n$?
  1. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2^2 + 2\,\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w + \|\boldsymbol w\|_2^2$
  2. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2^2 + \|\boldsymbol w\|_2^2$
  3. $(\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 + \|\boldsymbol w\|_2)^2$
  4. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2^2 + \boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w + \|\boldsymbol w\|_2^2$
Explanation. $(\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w)'(\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w) = \boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol p + 2\,\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w + \boldsymbol w'\boldsymbol w$, since $\boldsymbol w'\boldsymbol p = \boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w$. Without the cross term it holds only for orthogonal vectors.
la-f-norms-k51 · level facile · notes
By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, $|\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w| \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_2\,\|\boldsymbol w\|_2$. If $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = 3$ and $\|\boldsymbol w\|_2 = 2$, which value can the scalar product $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w$ not take?
  1. $7$
  2. $-6$
  3. $0$
  4. $6$
Explanation. It must be $-6 \le \boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol w \le 6$: $7$ is excluded. $\pm 6$ are obtained with parallel vectors, $0$ with orthogonal vectors.
la-f-norms-k52 · level facile · notes
By the reverse triangle inequality, $\big|\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 - \|\boldsymbol w\|_2\big| \le \|\boldsymbol p - \boldsymbol w\|_2$. If $\|\boldsymbol p\|_2 = 5$ and $\|\boldsymbol w\|_2 = 2$, what is the smallest possible value of $\|\boldsymbol p - \boldsymbol w\|_2$?
  1. $3$
  2. $7$
  3. $0$
  4. $\sqrt{21}$
Explanation. $\|\boldsymbol p - \boldsymbol w\|_2 \ge 5 - 2 = 3$, and the value $3$ is reached with $\boldsymbol w$ parallel to $\boldsymbol p$ with the same direction. $7$ is the maximum, not the minimum.
la-f-norms-k53 · level facile · notes
The generalized $\ell_2$ norm is $\|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q} = \sqrt{\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol p}$. With $\boldsymbol Q = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$ (symmetric positive definite) and $\boldsymbol p = (1, -1)'$, what is $\|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $\sqrt 2$
  3. $\sqrt 6$
  4. $2$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol p = (1, -1)'$ and $\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol p = 1 + 1 = 2$, hence $\sqrt 2$. $\sqrt 6$ ignores the signs of the mixed terms $q_{12}\,p_1 p_2 = -1$.
la-f-norms-k54 · level facile · notes
Let $\boldsymbol Q \in \R^{2\times 2}$ be symmetric positive definite and $\|\boldsymbol x\|_{\boldsymbol Q} = \sqrt{\boldsymbol x'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol x}$. What is the shape of the set $\{\boldsymbol x \in \R^2 : \|\boldsymbol x\|_{\boldsymbol Q} \le 1\}$?
  1. A half-plane bounded by a line
  2. A filled ellipse centred at the origin
  3. A filled diamond centred at the origin
  4. A square with sides parallel to the axes
Explanation. The axes of the ellipse are given by the eigenvectors of $\boldsymbol Q$, the lengths of the semi-axes by the eigenvalues. The diamond is the $\ell_1$ ball, the square the $\ell_\infty$ one.
la-f-norms-k55 · level facile · notes
The $\ell_\infty$ norm is $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty = \max_j |p_j|$. For which $t \in \R$ does the vector $(t, 3, -1)'$ have $\|(t, 3, -1)'\|_\infty = 3$?
  1. $t \le 3$
  2. $-3 \le t \le 3$
  3. Only $t = 3$
  4. Only $t = \pm 3$
Explanation. The maximum of $|t|$, $3$ and $1$ equals $3$ exactly when $|t| \le 3$. It suffices that the component $3$ is the largest in absolute value; $t \le 3$ would allow $t = -5$.
la-f-norms-k56 · level facile · notes
For every $\boldsymbol p \in \R^n$ we have $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_1 \le n\,\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty$ (with $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = \sum_j |p_j|$, $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty = \max_j |p_j|$). If $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty = 2$, what is known about $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1$?
  1. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = 2n$
  2. $0 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_1 \le 2$
  3. $2 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_1 \le 2n$
  4. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 \le 2$
Explanation. From the chain: $2 \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_1 \le 2n$. The bounds are reached with $\boldsymbol p = (2, 0, \dots, 0)'$ and with $\boldsymbol p = (2, \dots, 2)'$.
la-f-norms-k57 · level facile · notes
With the norm $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = \sum_j |p_j|$, for which pair does the equality $\|\boldsymbol p + \boldsymbol w\|_1 = \|\boldsymbol p\|_1 + \|\boldsymbol w\|_1$ hold?
  1. $\boldsymbol p = (1, 2)'$, $\boldsymbol w = (3, 0)'$
  2. $\boldsymbol p = (1, 2)'$, $\boldsymbol w = (-1, 0)'$
  3. $\boldsymbol p = (1, -2)'$, $\boldsymbol w = (0, 3)'$
  4. $\boldsymbol p = (2, 1)'$, $\boldsymbol w = (-2, -1)'$
Explanation. $(1, 2)' + (3, 0)' = (4, 2)'$: $6 = 3 + 3$. Equality holds when no component has opposite signs; the other pairs give $2 < 4$, $2 < 6$, $0 < 6$.
la-f-norms-k58 · level facile · notes
Let $\boldsymbol Q$ be symmetric positive definite, $\|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q} = \sqrt{\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol p}$, with $\|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q} = 2$ and $\|\boldsymbol w\|_{\boldsymbol Q} = 3$. What does the generalized Cauchy–Schwarz inequality guarantee?
  1. $|\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol w| = 6$
  2. $|\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol w| \ge 6$
  3. $|\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol w| \le 6$
  4. $|\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol w| \le 5$
Explanation. $|\boldsymbol p'\boldsymbol Q\boldsymbol w| \le \|\boldsymbol p\|_{\boldsymbol Q}\,\|\boldsymbol w\|_{\boldsymbol Q} = 2 \cdot 3 = 6$: it is an upper bound (product, not sum), reached only for parallel vectors.
la-f-norms-k59 · level teorema · notes
A norm must satisfy $\|\lambda\,\boldsymbol p\| = |\lambda|\,\|\boldsymbol p\|$. Which statement about the function $f(\boldsymbol p) = \|\boldsymbol p\|_2^2 = \sum_j p_j^2$ on $\R^n$ is correct?
  1. It is not a norm: $f(2\boldsymbol p) = 4 f(\boldsymbol p)$
  2. It is a norm, since $f(\boldsymbol p) \ge 0$
  3. It is a norm, since $f(\boldsymbol 0) = 0$
  4. It is the $\ell_1$ norm of $\boldsymbol p$
Explanation. $f(\lambda\boldsymbol p) = \lambda^2 f(\boldsymbol p)$, not $|\lambda| f(\boldsymbol p)$: homogeneity fails (and so does the triangle inequality, e.g. with $\boldsymbol w = \boldsymbol p \neq \boldsymbol 0$). Positivity and $f(\boldsymbol 0) = 0$ are not enough.
la-f-norms-k60 · level teorema · notes
Let $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = \sum_j |p_j|$ and $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty = \max_j |p_j|$. For which $\boldsymbol p \in \R^n$ does $\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty = \|\boldsymbol p\|_1$ hold?
  1. For those with all components equal
  2. For all of them, since both are norms
  3. For those with at most one nonzero component
  4. Only for the zero vector $\boldsymbol p = \boldsymbol 0$
Explanation. $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 - \|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty$ is the sum of the $|p_j|$ other than the maximum: it is zero exactly when at most one component is nonzero. With equal nonzero components one has $\|\boldsymbol p\|_1 = n\,\|\boldsymbol p\|_\infty$.

Part: Linear systems (64)

6. System of linear equations (64)

la-f-sys-01 · level facile · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{m\times n}$, $\boldsymbol b \in \R^m$ and $(\boldsymbol A | \boldsymbol b)$ the augmented matrix. By the Rouché–Capelli theorem, the system $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ has no solution if and only if:
  1. $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A) < n$ and $m \ge n$
  2. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 0$, with $m = n$
  3. $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A) < \text{rank}(\boldsymbol A | \boldsymbol b)$
  4. $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A) = \text{rank}(\boldsymbol A | \boldsymbol b) < n$
Explanation. The system has solutions if and only if the two ranks coincide. $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A) < n$ or $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 0$ exclude uniqueness, but the system may have infinitely many solutions.
la-f-sys-02 · level facile · notes
A system $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ with $n = 4$ variables has $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A) = \text{rank}(\boldsymbol A | \boldsymbol b) = 2$, where $(\boldsymbol A | \boldsymbol b)$ is the augmented matrix. How many solutions does it have?
  1. infinitely many, with $2$ free parameters
  2. infinitely many, with $4$ free parameters
  3. exactly one, since the ranks coincide
  4. none, since the rank is less than $4$
Explanation. The ranks coincide, so it is consistent; they are smaller than $n$, so there are infinitely many solutions with $n - r = 4 - 2 = 2$ free parameters.
la-f-sys-03 · level teorema · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{m\times n}$, $\boldsymbol b \in \R^m$ and $(\boldsymbol A|\boldsymbol b)$ the augmented matrix. Which statement is correct?
  1. $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ has a unique solution iff $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A) = \text{rank}(\boldsymbol A|\boldsymbol b) = n$
  2. $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ has a unique solution iff $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A) = n$
  3. if $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A|\boldsymbol b) = n$ the system has a unique solution
  4. if $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A) < n$ the system has no solution
Explanation. With $m > n$ it can happen that $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A) = n$ and $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A|\boldsymbol b) = n+1$: no solution. With $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A) < n$ the system may have infinitely many solutions.
la-f-sys-04 · level facile · notes
A homogeneous system $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol 0$:
  1. has solutions only if $\boldsymbol A$ is square
  2. always has the trivial solution $\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol 0$
  3. may have no solution at all
  4. always has infinitely many distinct solutions
Explanation. $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol 0 = \boldsymbol 0$ for every $\boldsymbol A$; nontrivial solutions exist if and only if $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A) < n$.
la-f-sys-05 · level teorema · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$. When does the homogeneous system $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol 0$ have nontrivial solutions?
  1. if and only if $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$
  2. if and only if $\boldsymbol A$ is symmetric
  3. never
  4. if and only if $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 0$
Explanation. Nontrivial solutions exist if and only if $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A) < n$, i.e., for a square matrix, if and only if $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 0$.
la-f-sys-06 · level facile · notes
A homogeneous system with $m = 2$ equations and $n = 3$ variables:
  1. may have no solution
  2. has nontrivial solutions only if $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 0$
  3. always has nontrivial solutions
  4. has only the trivial solution
Explanation. $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A) \le m = 2 < 3 = n$, hence there are infinitely many solutions with at least one free parameter. The determinant is not defined for a $2\times 3$ matrix.
la-f-sys-07 · level facile · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{m\times m}$ with $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$ and $\boldsymbol b \in \R^m$. In Cramer's rule, the entry $\tilde x_j$ of the solution of $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ is:
  1. $\det(\boldsymbol A_j)$, where $\boldsymbol A_j$ is $\boldsymbol A$ with column $j$ replaced by $\boldsymbol b$
  2. $\frac{\det(\boldsymbol A_j)}{\det(\boldsymbol A)}$, where $\boldsymbol A_j$ is $\boldsymbol A$ with column $j$ replaced by $\boldsymbol b$
  3. $\frac{\det(\boldsymbol A_j)}{\det(\boldsymbol A)}$, where $\boldsymbol A_j$ is $\boldsymbol A$ with row $j$ replaced by $\boldsymbol b'$
  4. $\frac{\det(\boldsymbol A)}{\det(\boldsymbol A_j)}$, where $\boldsymbol A_j$ is $\boldsymbol A$ with column $j$ replaced by $\boldsymbol b$
Explanation. Column $j$ (the coefficients of $x_j$) is replaced by $\boldsymbol b$ and the result is divided by $\det(\boldsymbol A)$; without the division the result is right only if $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 1$.
la-f-sys-08 · level facile · notes
By Cramer's rule, what is the first entry $\tilde x_1$ of the solution of the system $-x_1 + x_2 = 2$, $8x_1 + 2x_2 = 19$?
  1. $\frac{7}{2}$
  2. $-\frac{3}{2}$
  3. $\frac{2}{3}$
  4. $\frac{3}{2}$
Explanation. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = (-1)\cdot 2 - 1\cdot 8 = -10$, $\det(\boldsymbol A_1) = 2\cdot 2 - 1\cdot 19 = -15$, hence $\tilde x_1 = \frac{-15}{-10} = \frac{3}{2}$; $\frac{7}{2}$ is $\tilde x_2$.
la-f-sys-09 · level facile · notes
Why is Cramer's rule not used to solve systems $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ with $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{m\times m}$ and large $m$?
  1. it holds only if $\boldsymbol A$ is symmetric, while Gauss works for every nonsingular $\boldsymbol A$
  2. it holds only for $m \le 3$, because the determinants are computed with the Sarrus rule
  3. it needs $m+1$ determinants of order $m$: about $m!$ operations with Laplace, against $\frac{2}{3}m^3$ for Gauss
  4. it gives only an approximate solution, because it divides by $\det(\boldsymbol A)$, which may be small
Explanation. The formula is exact and holds for every $m$ with $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$, but it is expensive: with $m = 10$ we compare $10! = 3\,628\,800$ with about $667$ operations.
la-f-sys-10 · level facile · notes
The upper triangular system $\tilde{\boldsymbol A}\boldsymbol x = \tilde{\boldsymbol b}$, with $\tilde{\boldsymbol A} \in \R^{n\times n}$ and $\tilde a_{ii} \neq 0$, is solved by backward substitution, computing $x_n, x_{n-1}, \dots, x_1$. Which formula gives $x_i$?
  1. $x_i = \frac{\tilde b_i - \sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\tilde a_{ij}x_j}{\tilde a_{ii}}$
  2. $x_i = \tilde b_i - \sum_{j=i+1}^{n}\tilde a_{ij}x_j$
  3. $x_i = \frac{\tilde b_i}{\tilde a_{ii}}$
  4. $x_i = \frac{\tilde b_i - \sum_{j=i+1}^{n}\tilde a_{ij}x_j}{\tilde a_{ii}}$
Explanation. We proceed bottom-up: when $x_i$ is computed, $x_{i+1}, \dots, x_n$ are already known. The sum over $j < i$ is that of forward substitution.
la-f-sys-11 · level facile · notes
The solutions of a system $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ with one free parameter are written $\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol x_0 + t\,\boldsymbol v$, $t \in \R$. What are $\boldsymbol x_0$ and $\boldsymbol v$?
  1. $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x_0 = \boldsymbol b$ and $\boldsymbol v \neq \boldsymbol 0$ with $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol v = \boldsymbol 0$
  2. $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x_0 = \boldsymbol 0$ and $\boldsymbol v \neq \boldsymbol 0$ with $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol v = \boldsymbol b$
  3. $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x_0 = \boldsymbol b$ and $\boldsymbol v \neq \boldsymbol 0$ with $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol v = \boldsymbol b$
  4. $\boldsymbol x_0 = \boldsymbol 0$ and $\boldsymbol v \neq \boldsymbol 0$ with $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol v = \boldsymbol b$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol x_0$ is a particular solution (for $t = 0$) and $\boldsymbol v$ solves the homogeneous system: $\boldsymbol A(\boldsymbol x_0 + t\boldsymbol v) = \boldsymbol b + t\,\boldsymbol 0 = \boldsymbol b$.
la-f-sys-12 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x_0 = \boldsymbol b$ and $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol v = \boldsymbol 0$. Which property is needed to conclude that $\boldsymbol x_0 + \boldsymbol v$ is also a solution of $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$?
  1. the commutativity of the matrix product, $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol B = \boldsymbol B\boldsymbol A$
  2. the distributivity of the product over the sum: $\boldsymbol A(\boldsymbol x_0 + \boldsymbol v) = \boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x_0 + \boldsymbol A\boldsymbol v$
  3. Cramer's rule, applied to the system $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ entry by entry
  4. the invertibility of $\boldsymbol A$, i.e. $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$, which makes the solution unique
Explanation. $\boldsymbol A(\boldsymbol x_0 + \boldsymbol v) = \boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x_0 + \boldsymbol A\boldsymbol v = \boldsymbol b + \boldsymbol 0 = \boldsymbol b$. $\boldsymbol A$ need not be invertible: indeed, with $\boldsymbol v \neq \boldsymbol 0$ it is not.
la-f-sys-13 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{m\times n}$. Which result is needed to prove that $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol 0$ has nontrivial solutions if and only if $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A) < n$?
  1. Rouché–Capelli, with $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A | \boldsymbol 0) = \text{rank}(\boldsymbol A)$
  2. Cramer's rule applied to $\boldsymbol A$
  3. the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality on the rows
  4. the LU factorization without row swaps
Explanation. The system is always consistent (the zero column does not increase the rank); the solution is unique, hence only the trivial one, if and only if the rank is $n$.
la-f-sys-14 · level facile · notes
Forward elimination is applied to the augmented matrix $(\boldsymbol A | \boldsymbol b)$ of a system in $3$ variables; the last row becomes $(0\;\; 0\;\; 0 \mid 1)$. What can be concluded?
  1. the system has no solution
  2. the system has infinitely many solutions
  3. the system has a unique solution with $x_3 = 1$
  4. two columns must be swapped
Explanation. The row corresponds to the equation $0x_1 + 0x_2 + 0x_3 = 1$, which is impossible: $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A) < \text{rank}(\boldsymbol A|\boldsymbol b)$.
la-f-sys-15 · level facile · notes
Many systems $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ with the same $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n\times n}$ and different right-hand sides $\boldsymbol b$ must be solved. What is the advantage of the factorization $\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$?
  1. the factorization ($O(n^2)$) is done once; each new $\boldsymbol b$ costs two substitutions ($O(n)$)
  2. the factorization ($O(n^2)$) is redone for each $\boldsymbol b$; the two substitutions cost $O(n^3)$
  3. the factorization ($O(n^3)$) is done once; each new $\boldsymbol b$ costs an inversion ($O(n^3)$)
  4. the factorization ($O(n^3)$) is done once; each new $\boldsymbol b$ costs two substitutions ($O(n^2)$)
Explanation. The factorization takes about $\frac{2}{3}n^3$ operations and does not depend on $\boldsymbol b$; for each $\boldsymbol b$ forward and backward substitution are enough, each $O(n^2)$.
la-f-sys-16 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$. If $\boldsymbol y$ solves $\boldsymbol L\boldsymbol y = \boldsymbol b$ and $\boldsymbol x$ solves $\boldsymbol U\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol y$, which property guarantees that $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$?
  1. commutativity: $(\boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U)\boldsymbol x = (\boldsymbol U\boldsymbol L)\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol U\boldsymbol y = \boldsymbol b$
  2. the symmetry of $\boldsymbol A$, which makes $\boldsymbol U = \boldsymbol L'$ and $\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol y$
  3. the fact that $\det(\boldsymbol L) = 1$, which makes $\boldsymbol L\boldsymbol y = \boldsymbol y$
  4. associativity: $(\boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U)\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol L(\boldsymbol U\boldsymbol x) = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol y = \boldsymbol b$
Explanation. It is enough to group the factors differently: $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = (\boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U)\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol L(\boldsymbol U\boldsymbol x) = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol y = \boldsymbol b$. The matrix product is not commutative and $\det(\boldsymbol L) = 1$ does not mean $\boldsymbol L = \boldsymbol I$.
la-f-sys-17 · level facile · notes
A system $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ with $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{m\times n}$ and $m < n$ (fewer equations than variables):
  1. always has a unique solution
  2. never has solutions
  3. has a unique solution if $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$
  4. cannot have a unique solution
Explanation. $\text{rank}(\boldsymbol A) \le m < n$: if it is consistent it has infinitely many solutions, otherwise none. Uniqueness is possible only with $m \ge n$.
la-f-sys-18 · level facile · notes
$\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ is solved by Gaussian elimination (forward elimination, then backward substitution). What are the row operations of forward elimination applied to?
  1. the whole augmented matrix $(\boldsymbol A | \boldsymbol b)$, right-hand side column included
  2. only the matrix $\boldsymbol A$: the vector $\boldsymbol b$ is unchanged until the end
  3. only the vector $\boldsymbol b$: the matrix $\boldsymbol A$ is used to choose the multipliers
  4. the columns of $\boldsymbol A$, swapping variables and leaving $\boldsymbol b$ unchanged
Explanation. Each row operation turns an equation into an equivalent one, so it must be applied to both sides: one works on the augmented matrix and gets a triangular system $\tilde{\boldsymbol A}\boldsymbol x = \tilde{\boldsymbol b}$ with the same solutions.
la-f-systems-k01 · level facile · notes
A system $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ with $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{3 \times 5}$ has how many equations and unknowns?
  1. $5$ equations and $3$ unknowns
  2. $3$ equations and $15$ unknowns
  3. $8$ equations and $5$ unknowns
  4. $3$ equations and $5$ unknowns
Explanation. Rows = equations, columns = unknowns.
la-f-systems-k02 · level facile · notes
How many outcomes are possible for a linear system $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$?
  1. Always infinitely many
  2. None, one or infinitely many
  3. Only one or none
  4. Exactly two solutions or none
Explanation. Two distinct solutions generate infinitely many (the line through them).
la-f-systems-k03 · level facile · notes
In Gaussian elimination, on which matrix does one operate?
  1. On $\boldsymbol A^{-1}$ and then on $\boldsymbol b$
  2. On the matrix $(\boldsymbol A \,|\, \boldsymbol b)$
  3. Only on $\boldsymbol A$, then $\boldsymbol b$ is corrected
  4. On the transpose $\boldsymbol A'$
Explanation. Row operations must be applied to the right-hand sides too.
la-f-systems-k04 · level facile · notes
After forward elimination the system is $x_1 + x_2 = 5$, $2x_2 = 4$. Which solution does back substitution give?
  1. $(2, 3)$
  2. $(5, 2)$
  3. $(1, 4)$
  4. $(3, 2)$
Explanation. $x_2 = 2$, then $x_1 = 5 - 2 = 3$.
la-f-systems-k05 · level facile · notes
In Gaussian elimination on $\begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 & | & 3 \\ 6 & 4 & | & 10 \end{pmatrix}$, which operation zeroes the entry in position $(2,1)$?
  1. $R_2 \leftarrow 3R_2 - R_1$
  2. $R_1 \leftarrow R_1 - 3R_2$
  3. $R_2 \leftarrow R_2 - 3R_1$
  4. $R_2 \leftarrow R_2 + 3R_1$
Explanation. Multiplier $6/2 = 3$.
la-f-systems-k06 · level facile · notes
After forward elimination the augmented matrix of a system in $3$ unknowns is $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 1 & | & 3 \\ 0 & 1 & -1 & | & 2 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & | & 0 \end{pmatrix}$. How many solutions does the system have?
  1. Infinitely many, with $1$ free parameter
  2. None: there is a zero row
  3. Exactly one, since $\boldsymbol A$ is $3 \times 3$
  4. Infinitely many, with $2$ free parameters
Explanation. There are no rows $(0 \; 0 \; 0 \,|\, c)$ with $c \neq 0$ and both ranks are $2 < 3$: infinitely many solutions with $3 - 2 = 1$ free parameter ($x_3$). The zero row does not make the system inconsistent.
la-f-systems-k07 · level facile · notes
An echelon form of $(\boldsymbol A|\boldsymbol b)$, with unknowns $x_1, \dots, x_4$, has pivots in columns $1$ and $3$ and no row $(0 \cdots 0 \,|\, c)$ with $c \neq 0$. Which unknowns are free parameters?
  1. Only $x_4$, the last one
  2. None of them
  3. $x_2$ and $x_4$
  4. $x_1$ and $x_3$
Explanation. The unknowns of the pivot columns ($x_1$, $x_3$) are found by back substitution; the other $4 - 2 = 2$, i.e. $x_2$ and $x_4$, are free.
la-f-systems-k08 · level teorema · notes
What does the Rouché–Capelli theorem state for $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ with $n$ unknowns?
  1. It always has infinitely many solutions if $m < n$
  2. Consistent iff $\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A) = \mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A|\boldsymbol b)$
  3. It has solutions iff $\boldsymbol A$ is square
  4. It has solutions iff $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$
Explanation. Otherwise the solutions depend on $n - \mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A)$ parameters.
la-f-systems-k09 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about a system with more unknowns than equations ($m < n$) is correct?
  1. It is always inconsistent
  2. It always has a unique solution
  3. It cannot have a unique solution
  4. It always has infinitely many solutions
Explanation. $\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A) \le m < n$: if consistent, infinitely many solutions; it may also be inconsistent.
la-f-systems-k10 · level ipotesi · notes
To conclude that $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ with $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n \times n}$ has a unique solution for every $\boldsymbol b$, which assumption suffices?
  1. $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$
  2. $\boldsymbol b \neq \boldsymbol 0$
  3. $\boldsymbol A$ symmetric
  4. $\boldsymbol A$ with positive entries
Explanation. Rank $n$ for $\boldsymbol A$ and $(\boldsymbol A|\boldsymbol b)$: unique solution $\boldsymbol A^{-1}\boldsymbol b$.
la-f-systems-k11 · level facile · notes
Which system is homogeneous?
  1. $x_1 = 2$, $x_2 = 0$
  2. $x_1 + x_2 = 1$, $x_1 - x_2 = 0$
  3. $x_1 - 2x_2 = 0$, $3x_1 + x_2 = 0$
  4. $x_1 - 2x_2 = 1$, $3x_1 + x_2 = 0$
Explanation. All right-hand sides are zero: $\boldsymbol b = \boldsymbol 0$.
la-f-systems-k12 · level facile · notes
The homogeneous system $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol 0$ has $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{4\times 6}$ and $\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A) = 3$. How many free parameters do its solutions have?
  1. $2$
  2. $0$
  3. $3$
  4. $1$
Explanation. The solutions have $n - \mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A) = 6 - 3 = 3$ free parameters ($n$: number of unknowns). $1 = 4 - 3$ uses the number of equations.
la-f-systems-k13 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about homogeneous systems $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol 0$ with $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{m \times n}$ is correct?
  1. They have nontrivial solutions iff $\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A) < n$
  2. They always have only the trivial solution
  3. They have nontrivial solutions iff $m = n$
  4. They never have nontrivial solutions if $m \ge n$
Explanation. With rank $n$ the solution is unique, hence the zero one.
la-f-systems-k14 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that the solutions of $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol 0$ form a set closed under addition and scalar multiplication, what is used?
  1. That $\boldsymbol A$ is square ($m = n$)
  2. That the solutions are positive
  3. The linearity of the product $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x$
  4. That $\boldsymbol A$ is invertible
Explanation. Combinations of solutions still give $\boldsymbol 0$; with $\boldsymbol b \neq \boldsymbol 0$ it fails.
la-f-systems-k15 · level facile · notes
The homogeneous system $x_1 + 2x_2 = 0$, $2x_1 + 4x_2 = 0$ has:
  1. exactly two solutions
  2. infinitely many solutions $(-2t, t)$
  3. only the zero solution
  4. no solution, it is inconsistent
Explanation. The equations are proportional: rank $1 < 2$.
la-f-systems-k16 · level facile · notes
With $\boldsymbol A = \boldsymbol L\boldsymbol U$, $\boldsymbol L = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ and $\boldsymbol b = (3, 8)'$, what is $\boldsymbol y$ in $\boldsymbol L\boldsymbol y = \boldsymbol b$?
  1. $(2, 3)'$
  2. $(3, 2)'$
  3. $(3, 8)'$
  4. $(3, 14)'$
Explanation. Forward substitution: $y_1 = 3$, $y_2 = 8 - 2 \cdot 3 = 2$.
la-f-systems-k17 · level ipotesi · notes
Why is the LU factorization convenient when solving many systems $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b_k$ with the same $\boldsymbol A$?
  1. Because it holds only for $\boldsymbol b = \boldsymbol 0$
  2. One factorizes only once
  3. Because it makes $\boldsymbol A$ symmetric
  4. Because it avoids elimination entirely
Explanation. The cost of the factorization is amortized.
la-f-systems-k18 · level facile · notes
With Cramer's rule, what is $x_1$ in the system $2x_1 + x_2 = 5$, $x_1 - x_2 = 1$?
  1. $1$
  2. $-2$
  3. $3$
  4. $2$
Explanation. $\det\boldsymbol A = -3$, $\det\boldsymbol A_1 = \det\begin{pmatrix} 5 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{pmatrix} = -6$: $x_1 = 2$.
la-f-systems-k19 · level facile · notes
In Cramer's rule, how is the matrix $\boldsymbol A_2$ built?
  1. By replacing the second row of $\boldsymbol A$ with $\boldsymbol b'$
  2. By deleting row and column $2$
  3. By multiplying the second column by $\boldsymbol b$
  4. By replacing the second column of $\boldsymbol A$ with $\boldsymbol b$
Explanation. $x_j = \det(\boldsymbol A_j)/\det(\boldsymbol A)$.
la-f-systems-k20 · level facile · notes
For a $3 \times 3$ system with $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 4$ and $\det(\boldsymbol A_3) = -8$, what is $x_3$?
  1. $-\frac12$
  2. $-2$
  3. $2$
  4. $-32$
Explanation. $x_3 = \det(\boldsymbol A_3)/\det(\boldsymbol A)$.
la-f-systems-k21 · level facile · notes
To apply Cramer's rule to a system of three equations in three unknowns, how many $3 \times 3$ determinants are needed?
  1. $3$
  2. $1$
  3. $9$
  4. $4$
Explanation. $\det(\boldsymbol A)$ and $\det(\boldsymbol A_1), \det(\boldsymbol A_2), \det(\boldsymbol A_3)$.
la-f-systems-k22 · level facile · notes
With Cramer's rule for the $3 \times 3$ system $x_1 = 1$, $x_2 = 2$, $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 = 6$, what is $\det(\boldsymbol A)$?
  1. $3$
  2. $6$
  3. $1$
  4. $0$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol A$ is lower triangular with diagonal $(1, 1, 1)$.
la-f-systems-k23 · level facile · notes
Why is Cramer's rule not used for large systems?
  1. Because it holds only for $m = 2$
  2. Because it requires $\boldsymbol b = \boldsymbol 0$
  3. It costs like $m!$ with Laplace
  4. Because it does not give the exact solution
Explanation. Gaussian elimination instead costs about $m^3$ operations.
la-f-systems-k24 · level facile · notes
Which method is preferable to solve a $1000 \times 1000$ system with a nonsingular matrix?
  1. Cramer's rule with Laplace expansion
  2. Enumerating all possible solutions
  3. Computing the adjugate of $\boldsymbol A$ and then $\boldsymbol A^{-1}$
  4. Gaussian elimination (or LU factorization)
Explanation. Polynomial cost against factorial cost.
la-f-systems-k25 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about Cramer's rule is correct?
  1. $x_j = \det(\boldsymbol A_j)/\det(\boldsymbol A)$ if $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$
  2. It holds for every system, even rectangular
  3. It holds also with $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 0$
  4. It gives $x_j = \det(\boldsymbol A)/\det(\boldsymbol A_j)$
Explanation. A unique solution and division by $\det(\boldsymbol A)$ are needed.
la-f-systems-k26 · level ipotesi · notes
Cramer's rule is proved by writing $\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol A^{-1}\boldsymbol b$ with the inverse via the adjugate. Which assumption is needed?
  1. $\boldsymbol A$ symmetric
  2. $m = 2$
  3. $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$
  4. $\boldsymbol b > \boldsymbol 0$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol A^{-1} = \mathrm{adj}(\boldsymbol A)/\det(\boldsymbol A)$ requires a nonzero determinant.
la-f-systems-k27 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about Gaussian elimination is correct?
  1. It changes the solutions but not their number
  2. It works only for square matrices
  3. It requires the right-hand side $\boldsymbol b = \boldsymbol 0$
  4. Row operations do not change the solutions
Explanation. Each operation is invertible, so the system stays equivalent.
la-f-systems-k28 · level ipotesi · notes
Back substitution on an $n \times n$ upper triangular system gives a unique solution under which assumption?
  1. That the right-hand side is $\boldsymbol b \neq \boldsymbol 0$
  2. That the size is $n \le 3$
  3. All diagonal entries are nonzero
  4. All entries of the matrix are positive
Explanation. Each diagonal entry is a divisor.
la-f-systems-k29 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the LU method for $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ is correct?
  1. Solve only $\boldsymbol L\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$, forward, without $\boldsymbol U$
  2. Solve $\boldsymbol L\boldsymbol y = \boldsymbol b$ forward and then $\boldsymbol U\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol y$ backward
  3. Solve $\boldsymbol U\boldsymbol y = \boldsymbol b$ and then $\boldsymbol L\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol y$, both forward
  4. Compute $\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol L^{-1}\boldsymbol U^{-1}\boldsymbol b$ with the inverses
Explanation. $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol L(\boldsymbol U\boldsymbol x) = \boldsymbol b$.
la-f-systems-k30 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the solutions of $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ is correct?
  1. Twice a solution is a solution
  2. They are $\boldsymbol x_0 + \boldsymbol h$ with $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol h = \boldsymbol 0$
  3. The solutions always form a subspace
  4. The sum of two solutions is a solution
Explanation. A particular solution plus the solutions of the associated homogeneous system.
la-f-systems-k31 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that a system with two distinct solutions has infinitely many, what is used?
  1. That the solutions are integers
  2. $\boldsymbol x_1 + t(\boldsymbol x_2 - \boldsymbol x_1)$ is a solution
  3. That $\boldsymbol A$ is square and invertible
  4. That the right-hand side is $\boldsymbol b = \boldsymbol 0$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol A(\boldsymbol x_2 - \boldsymbol x_1) = \boldsymbol 0$.
la-f-systems-k32 · level facile · notes
$\boldsymbol v = (1, 2, -1)'$ and $\boldsymbol w = (0, 1, 1)'$ solve the homogeneous system $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol 0$, with $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{2\times 3}$. Which vector surely solves it?
  1. $(2,\, 1,\, -5)'$
  2. $(2,\, 2,\, -1)'$
  3. $(1,\, 1,\, 1)'$
  4. $(0,\, 0,\, 1)'$
Explanation. $(2, 1, -5)' = 2\boldsymbol v - 3\boldsymbol w$ and $\boldsymbol A(2\boldsymbol v - 3\boldsymbol w) = 2\cdot\boldsymbol 0 - 3\cdot\boldsymbol 0 = \boldsymbol 0$. The others are not linear combinations of $\boldsymbol v$ and $\boldsymbol w$.
la-f-systems-k33 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about a square homogeneous system $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol 0$ with $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{n \times n}$ is correct?
  1. It has only the zero solution iff $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$
  2. It always has nonzero solutions
  3. It has no solution if $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 0$
  4. It has nonzero solutions iff $\boldsymbol A$ is symmetric
Explanation. Rank $n$ iff nonzero determinant.
la-f-systems-k34 · level ipotesi · notes
To conclude that the homogeneous system $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol 0$ with $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{3 \times 3}$ and $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 0$ has infinitely many solutions, what is used?
  1. That $\boldsymbol A$ is upper triangular
  2. That the right-hand side is $\boldsymbol b \neq \boldsymbol 0$
  3. Cramer's rule on $\boldsymbol A$
  4. Rouché–Capelli with rank $< 3$
Explanation. Consistent because of the zero solution, with $3 - \mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A) \ge 1$ parameters.
la-f-systems-k35 · level facile · notes
Cramer's rule for $3x_1 + x_2 = 7$, $x_1 + 2x_2 = 4$ ($\boldsymbol A_j$: $\boldsymbol A$ with column $j$ replaced by $\boldsymbol b$). What are $\det(\boldsymbol A)$ and $\det(\boldsymbol A_2)$?
  1. $5$ and $10$
  2. $7$ and $5$
  3. $5$ and $-5$
  4. $5$ and $5$
Explanation. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 3 \cdot 2 - 1 \cdot 1 = 5$, $\det(\boldsymbol A_2) = 3 \cdot 4 - 7 \cdot 1 = 5$, so $x_2 = 1$ (and $x_1 = 2$). $10$ is $\det(\boldsymbol A_1)$.
la-f-systems-k36 · level facile · notes
Cramer's rule $\tilde x_j = \det(\boldsymbol A_j)/\det(\boldsymbol A)$ holds if $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$. What happens if it is applied to the system $x_1 + 2x_2 = 3$, $2x_1 + 4x_2 = 5$?
  1. It gives $x_1 = 1$ and $x_2 = 1$
  2. It does not apply: $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 0$
  3. It gives $x_1 = 3$ and $x_2 = 0$
  4. It gives $x_1 = x_2 = 0$
Explanation. $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 1 \cdot 4 - 2 \cdot 2 = 0$: the formula divides by zero. (The system is in fact inconsistent: the second equation would give $2 \cdot 3 = 5$.)
la-f-systems-k37 · level facile · notes
In Cramer's rule $\boldsymbol A_j$ is $\boldsymbol A$ with column $j$ replaced by $\boldsymbol b$. For the system $x_1 + x_2 = 1$, $x_2 + x_3 = 2$, $x_1 + x_3 = 3$, which matrix is $\boldsymbol A_3$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 0 \\ 2 & 1 & 1 \\ 3 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 & 2 \\ 1 & 0 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. $\boldsymbol A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ and $\boldsymbol b = (1, 2, 3)'$ takes the place of the third column. Replacing the third row is a mistake; the third option is $\boldsymbol A_1$.
la-f-systems-k38 · level facile · notes
For a system of three equations in three unknowns one finds $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 0$ and $\det(\boldsymbol A_1) = 5$ ($\boldsymbol A_1$: first column replaced by $\boldsymbol b$). What can be said with Cramer's rule?
  1. It does not apply: $\det(\boldsymbol A) = 0$
  2. It gives $x_1 = 0$, like $\det(\boldsymbol A)$
  3. It gives $x_1 = 5$, like $\det(\boldsymbol A_1)$
  4. It gives $x_1 = \frac15$, the inverse of $5$
Explanation. The formula $\tilde x_1 = \det(\boldsymbol A_1)/\det(\boldsymbol A)$ requires $\det(\boldsymbol A) \neq 0$; here it gives no value. $\frac15$ inverts the ratio.
la-f-systems-k39 · level facile · notes
For a system of $m$ equations in $m$ unknowns, how does the number of operations of Gaussian elimination grow, roughly, as $m$ grows?
  1. Like $2^m$
  2. Like $m$
  3. Like $m^3$
  4. Like $m!$
Explanation. Gaussian elimination needs about $\frac23 m^3$ operations. $m!$ is the growth of the determinant with the Laplace expansion, used by Cramer's rule.
la-f-systems-k40 · level facile · notes
Even computing every determinant with Gaussian elimination, why does Cramer's rule for an $m \times m$ system remain more expensive than solving the system with Gauss?
  1. It needs $m!$ eliminations, one per permutation
  2. It needs $m + 1$ eliminations instead of one
  3. It needs to invert $\boldsymbol A$ twice
  4. It works only in exact arithmetic
Explanation. It needs $\det(\boldsymbol A), \det(\boldsymbol A_1), \dots, \det(\boldsymbol A_m)$: $m + 1$ determinants, each costing as much as one elimination, while Gauss on the system uses only one.
la-f-systems-k41 · level facile · notes
A system $\boldsymbol A\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol b$ with $\boldsymbol A \in \R^{4\times 3}$ has $\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A) = 3$ and $\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A|\boldsymbol b) = 4$ ($(\boldsymbol A|\boldsymbol b)$: augmented matrix). How many solutions does it have?
  1. Exactly one
  2. Infinitely many
  3. Exactly four
  4. None
Explanation. By the Rouché–Capelli theorem, $\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A) < \mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A|\boldsymbol b)$ means an inconsistent system. Full rank of $\boldsymbol A$ is not enough.
la-f-systems-k42 · level facile · notes
Let $(\boldsymbol A|\boldsymbol b)$ be the augmented matrix of a system, obtained by appending the column $\boldsymbol b$ to $\boldsymbol A$. If $\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A) = r$, which values can $\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A|\boldsymbol b)$ take?
  1. Only $r$
  2. $r$ or $r - 1$
  3. Any value between $r$ and $m$
  4. $r$ or $r + 1$
Explanation. Appending a column does not decrease the rank and increases it by at most $1$: $r \le \mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A|\boldsymbol b) \le r + 1$. This is why the three Rouché–Capelli cases cover everything.
la-f-systems-k43 · level facile · notes
After forward elimination one gets the triangular system $x_1 - x_2 + 2x_3 = 3$, $x_2 + x_3 = 4$, $2x_3 = 2$. With back substitution, what is $x_1$?
  1. $8$
  2. $4$
  3. $-2$
  4. $2$
Explanation. $x_3 = 1$, then $x_2 = 4 - 1 = 3$, finally $x_1 = 3 + x_2 - 2x_3 = 3 + 3 - 2 = 4$. $-2$ gets the sign of $x_2$ wrong ($3 - 3 - 2$).
la-f-systems-k44 · level facile · notes
At the end of forward elimination, $(\boldsymbol A|\boldsymbol b)$ is in row echelon form. How are $\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A)$ and $\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol A|\boldsymbol b)$ read off?
  1. By counting the pivots equal to $1$
  2. By counting the positive diagonal entries
  3. By adding up the pivot values
  4. By counting the nonzero rows
Explanation. The rank of an echelon matrix is the number of its nonzero rows (of $\boldsymbol A$ and of $(\boldsymbol A|\boldsymbol b)$). The pivots need not equal $1$ nor be positive.
la-f-systems-k45 · level facile · notes
LU method: once $\boldsymbol y$ is known from $\boldsymbol L\boldsymbol y = \boldsymbol b$, one solves $\boldsymbol U\boldsymbol x = \boldsymbol y$. With $\boldsymbol U = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 0 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$ and $\boldsymbol y = (5, 6)'$, what is $\boldsymbol x$?
  1. $(3,\, 2)'$
  2. $(\frac32,\, 2)'$
  3. $(\frac52,\, 2)'$
  4. $(2,\, \frac32)'$
Explanation. Backward: $x_2 = \frac63 = 2$, then $2x_1 + 2 = 5$, i.e. $x_1 = \frac32$. $(\frac52, 2)'$ forgets the term $u_{12}\,x_2$.
la-f-systems-k46 · level facile · notes
Forward elimination on the augmented matrix $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & | & 4 \\ 3 & 5 & | & 9 \end{pmatrix}$ ($R_i$: row $i$). Which row is obtained with $R_2 \leftarrow R_2 - 3R_1$?
  1. $(0 \;\; {-1} \;|\; 9)$
  2. $(0 \;\; 11 \;|\; 21)$
  3. $(0 \;\; {-1} \;|\; {-3})$
  4. $(0 \;\; 1 \;|\; 3)$
Explanation. $(3, 5 \,|\, 9) - 3\,(1, 2 \,|\, 4) = (0, -1 \,|\, -3)$. The operation must be applied to the right-hand side too; $(0, 11 \,|\, 21)$ adds instead of subtracting.

Part: Further topics (91)

A.1. Absolute value (45)

la-f-abs-01 · level facile · notes
For $\varepsilon > 0$, what is $|a| < \varepsilon$ equivalent to?
  1. $0 < a < \varepsilon$
  2. $-\varepsilon < a < \varepsilon$
  3. $a < \varepsilon$
  4. $a > -\varepsilon$ or $a < \varepsilon$
Explanation. It follows from the definition: $|a| < \varepsilon$ holds both for $a \ge 0$ with $a < \varepsilon$ and for $a < 0$ with $-a < \varepsilon$. Both inequalities are needed, together.
la-f-abs-02 · level teorema · notes
Which inequality holds for every $b, c \in \R$?
  1. $|b + c| = |b| + |c|$
  2. $|b - c| \le |b| - |c|$
  3. $|b + c| \le |b| + |c|$
  4. $|b + c| \ge |b| + |c|$
Explanation. It is the triangle inequality; equality holds only if $b$ and $c$ have the same sign (or one of them is zero). With $b = 1$, $c = -1$: $0 \le 2$.
la-f-abs-03 · level teorema · notes
Which inequality (reverse triangle inequality) holds for every $g, h \in \R$?
  1. $|g - h| \le \big|\,|g| - |h|\,\big|$
  2. $|g| - |h| \ge |g - h|$
  3. $\big|\,|g| - |h|\,\big| \ge |g + h|$
  4. $\big|\,|g| - |h|\,\big| \le |g - h|$
Explanation. The difference of the absolute values does not exceed the absolute value of the difference. With $g = 1$, $h = -1$: $0 \le 2$, while the second one would give $2 \le 0$.
la-f-abs-04 · level ipotesi · notes
How is the triangle inequality $|b + c| \le |b| + |c|$ proved for every $b, c \in \R$?
  1. start from the reverse triangle inequality $\big|\,|b| - |c|\,\big| \le |b - c|$ and replace $c$ with $-c$
  2. multiply $-|b| \le b \le |b|$ and $-|c| \le c \le |c|$, then use $|b\,c| = |b|\,|c|$
  3. square both sides and use $(b + c)^2 = b^2 + c^2$, then take the square root
  4. add $-|b| \le b \le |b|$ and $-|c| \le c \le |c|$, then use $|a| \le \varepsilon \Leftrightarrow -\varepsilon \le a \le \varepsilon$
Explanation. Adding member to member: $-(|b| + |c|) \le b + c \le |b| + |c|$; with $\varepsilon = |b| + |c| \ge 0$ and $a = b + c$ the claim follows. The reverse triangle inequality is derived from the triangle inequality, not conversely, and it gives a lower bound anyway.
la-f-abs-05 · level ipotesi · notes
Which steps prove the reverse triangle inequality $\big|\,|g| - |h|\,\big| \le |g - h|$, for every $g, h \in \R$?
  1. $|g| - |h| \le |g - h|$ (triangle inequality with $b = g - h$, $c = h$), the same with $g$ and $h$ swapped, then $|a| \le \varepsilon \Leftrightarrow -\varepsilon \le a \le \varepsilon$
  2. $|g| - |h| \le |g - h|$ (triangle inequality with $b = g - h$, $c = h$), which is enough alone because $|g| - |h| \ge 0$ always
  3. $|g\,h| = |g|\,|h|$ with $h$ replaced by $-h$, then $|a| \le \varepsilon \Leftrightarrow -\varepsilon \le a \le \varepsilon$ with $\varepsilon = |g - h|$
  4. the triangle inequality with $b = g$, $c = h$, i.e. $|g + h| \le |g| + |h|$, then replace $h$ with $|h|$
Explanation. The triangle inequality with $b = g-h$, $c = h$ gives $|g| - |h| \le |g-h|$; swapping $g$ and $h$, $|h| - |g| \le |g-h|$. Hence $-|g-h| \le |g| - |h| \le |g-h|$ and we conclude with $\varepsilon = |g-h|$. It is not true that $|g| - |h| \ge 0$ always.
la-f-abs-06 · level facile · notes
Which $x \in \R$ satisfy $|x - 2| \le 3$?
  1. $x \le 5$
  2. $-5 \le x \le 1$
  3. $x \le -1$ or $x \ge 5$
  4. $-1 \le x \le 5$
Explanation. $|x - 2| \le 3 \Leftrightarrow -3 \le x - 2 \le 3 \Leftrightarrow -1 \le x \le 5$. The last one is the solution of $|x - 2| \ge 3$.
la-f-abs-07 · level facile · notes
The absolute value is $|a| = a$ if $a \ge 0$ and $|a| = -a$ if $a < 0$. What is $|-7| - |3 - 5|$?
  1. $5$
  2. $9$
  3. $-9$
  4. $-5$
Explanation. $|-7| = 7$ and $|3 - 5| = |-2| = 2$, hence $7 - 2 = 5$. Writing $|3 - 5|$ as $-2$ would give $9$.
la-f-abs-08 · level facile · notes
Using $|a| < \varepsilon \Leftrightarrow -\varepsilon < a < \varepsilon$ (for $\varepsilon > 0$), which $x \in \R$ satisfy $|2x - 1| < 5$?
  1. $x < -2$ or $x > 3$
  2. $-3 < x < 2$
  3. $-2 < x < 3$
  4. $-4 < x < 6$
Explanation. $-5 < 2x - 1 < 5$, i.e. $-4 < 2x < 6$, i.e. $-2 < x < 3$. The set $x < -2$ or $x > 3$ is the one of $|2x - 1| > 5$.
la-f-abs-09 · level teorema · notes
Which inequality, a form of the triangle inequality, holds for every $d, e, f \in \R$?
  1. $|d - e| \ge |d - f| + |e - f|$
  2. $|d + e| \le |d - f| + |e - f|$
  3. $|d - e| \le |d - f| + |e - f|$
  4. $|d - e| \le |d - f| - |e - f|$
Explanation. Set $b = d - f$, $c = f - e$ in $|b + c| \le |b| + |c|$ and use $|f - e| = |e - f|$: the distance between $d$ and $e$ does not exceed the sum of the distances from a third point $f$.
la-f-abs-10 · level facile · notes
Let $b_1 = 3$, $b_2 = -5$, $b_3 = 1$. What are $\big|\sum_{i=1}^{3} b_i\big|$ and $\sum_{i=1}^{3} |b_i|$?
  1. $1$ and $9$
  2. $9$ and $9$
  3. $-1$ and $9$
  4. $1$ and $1$
Explanation. $|3 - 5 + 1| = |-1| = 1$ and $3 + 5 + 1 = 9$: as the generalized triangle inequality states, $\big|\sum_i b_i\big| \le \sum_i |b_i|$, here strictly.
la-f-abs-11 · level facile · notes
Which equality on the absolute value holds for every $b, c \in \R$?
  1. $|b - c| = |b| - |c|$
  2. $|-b| = -|b|$
  3. $|b\,c| = |b|\,|c|$
  4. $|b + c| = |b| + |c|$
Explanation. The absolute value is multiplicative. For the sum only the inequality $|b + c| \le |b| + |c|$ holds (for instance $b = 1$, $c = -1$), and $|-b| = |b|$.
la-f-abs-12 · level facile · notes
The absolute value is $|a| = a$ if $a \ge 0$ and $|a| = -a$ if $a < 0$. Which is the set of $x \in \R$ with $|x| = -x$?
  1. only $x = 0$
  2. no $x$
  3. $x \le 0$
  4. $x \ge 0$
Explanation. For $x < 0$ we have $|x| = -x$ by definition, and for $x = 0$ both sides are $0$; for $x > 0$ instead $|x| = x \neq -x$.
la-f-abs-k01 · level facile · notes
The absolute value is $|a| = a$ if $a \ge 0$ and $|a| = -a$ if $a < 0$. If $|a| = 3$, which values can the real $a$ take?
  1. Only the value $a = 3$
  2. Only the value $a = -3$
  3. Every $a$ with $-3 \le a \le 3$
  4. $a = 3$ or $a = -3$
Explanation. $|a| = 3$ holds for $a = 3$ (case $a \ge 0$) and for $a = -3$ (case $a < 0$, with $-a = 3$). The values in between have $|a| < 3$.
la-f-abs-k02 · level facile · notes
How is the absolute value $|x|$ of a real $x$ defined?
  1. $x^2$ for every $x$
  2. $\sqrt{x}$ if $x \ge 0$, $0$ otherwise
  3. $x$ if $x \ge 0$, $-x$ if $x < 0$
  4. $x$ if $x \le 0$, $-x$ if $x > 0$
Explanation. The absolute value changes the sign of negatives.
la-f-abs-k03 · level facile · notes
The absolute value is $|a| = a$ if $a \ge 0$ and $|a| = -a$ if $a < 0$. For which $x \in \R$ does $|x| \le x$ hold?
  1. $x \le 0$
  2. $x \ge 0$
  3. For every $x \in \R$
  4. For no $x$
Explanation. For $x \ge 0$ we have $|x| = x$; for $x < 0$ we have $|x| = -x > x$. So it holds exactly for $x \ge 0$ (and always $|x| \ge x$).
la-f-abs-k04 · level facile · notes
For which $x$ does $|x| > 4$ hold?
  1. $x > -4$
  2. $x < -4$ or $x > 4$
  3. $-4 < x < 4$
  4. $x > 4$
Explanation. Distance from $0$ greater than $4$: two half-lines.
la-f-abs-k05 · level facile · notes
What is $\sqrt{x^2}$ for a real $x$?
  1. $x^2$
  2. $|x|$
  3. $x$
  4. $-x$
Explanation. The square root is nonnegative.
la-f-abs-k06 · level facile · notes
What is $|{-2}| \cdot |3| - |{-6}|$?
  1. $0$
  2. $-12$
  3. $12$
  4. $6$
Explanation. $2 \cdot 3 - 6 = 0$.
la-f-abs-k07 · level facile · notes
With $x = 5$ and $y = -3$, which relation holds between $|x + y|$ and $|x| + |y|$?
  1. $2 < 8$
  2. $2 = 2$
  3. $8 < 2$
  4. $8 = 8$
Explanation. $|x + y| = 2$, $|x| + |y| = 8$: strict inequality for opposite signs.
la-f-abs-k08 · level facile · notes
When does the equality $|x + y| = |x| + |y|$ hold?
  1. Never
  2. When $x y \ge 0$
  3. Only when $x = y$
  4. Always
Explanation. With the same sign no term compensates the other.
la-f-abs-k09 · level facile · notes
With $x = 7$ and $y = 2$, what are $\big||x| - |y|\big|$ and $|x - y|$?
  1. $9$ and $5$
  2. $9$ and $9$
  3. $5$ and $5$
  4. $5$ and $9$
Explanation. Same sign: the reverse triangle inequality holds with equality.
la-f-abs-k10 · level facile · notes
Which statement about $|x - y|$ for two reals $x, y$ is correct?
  1. It always equals $|x| - |y|$
  2. It can be a negative number
  3. It always equals $x - y$
  4. The distance between $x$ and $y$
Explanation. The absolute value of the difference measures distance.
la-f-abs-k11 · level teorema · notes
What does the triangle inequality state for reals?
  1. $|x \cdot y| \le |x| + |y|$ for all $x, y$
  2. $|x + y| \le |x| + |y|$ for all $x, y$
  3. $|x + y| \ge |x| + |y|$ for all $x, y$
  4. $|x + y| = |x| + |y|$ for all $x, y$
Explanation. The absolute value of a sum does not exceed the sum of absolute values.
la-f-abs-k12 · level teorema · notes
What does the reverse triangle inequality state?
  1. $|x| - |y| \ge |x + y|$
  2. $\big||x| - |y|\big| \le |x - y|$
  3. $|x - y| \le \big||x| - |y|\big|$
  4. $|x - y| = |x| - |y|$
Explanation. Apply the triangle inequality to $x = (x - y) + y$ and to $y = (y - x) + x$.
la-f-abs-k13 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the absolute value is correct?
  1. $|x| = x$ for every $x \in \R$
  2. $|x\,y| = |x|\,|y|$ for all $x, y$
  3. $|x + y| = |x| + |y|$ for all $x, y$
  4. $|{-x}| = -|x|$ for every $x$
Explanation. The absolute value is multiplicative, but not additive.
la-f-abs-k14 · level ipotesi · notes
A proof of the triangle inequality adds $-|x| \le x \le |x|$ and $-|y| \le y \le |y|$. What is needed to conclude?
  1. That $x$ and $y$ are positive
  2. That $x$ and $y$ are equal
  3. That $|x + y| = x + y$ always
  4. That $-a \le t \le a$ means $|t| \le a$
Explanation. We get $-(|x| + |y|) \le x + y \le |x| + |y|$.
la-f-abs-k15 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove the reverse triangle inequality $\big||x| - |y|\big| \le |x - y|$, what is used?
  1. Triangle inequality, twice
  2. That $x \ge y$
  3. The definition of the $\ell_2$ norm
  4. That $|x - y| = |x| - |y|$
Explanation. $|x| \le |x - y| + |y|$ and $|y| \le |y - x| + |x|$.
la-f-abs-k16 · level ipotesi · notes
To solve $|x - 1| = 2x$, which remark is needed before splitting into cases?
  1. None: square without conditions
  2. The right-hand side must be $\ge 0$, so $x \ge 0$
  3. $x$ must be an integer number
  4. $x - 1$ must be positive, so $x > 1$
Explanation. An absolute value is never negative; then $x = \frac13$.
la-f-abs-k17 · level facile · notes
Using $|a| \le \varepsilon \Leftrightarrow -\varepsilon \le a \le \varepsilon$, which $x \in \R$ satisfy $|x - 1| \ge 2$?
  1. $-1 \le x \le 3$
  2. $x \le -3$ or $x \ge 1$
  3. $x \le -2$ or $x \ge 2$
  4. $x \le -1$ or $x \ge 3$
Explanation. $|x - 1| \ge 2$ is the negation of $-2 < x - 1 < 2$, i.e. of $-1 < x < 3$: the two half-lines $x \le -1$ and $x \ge 3$ remain. $x \le -3$ or $x \ge 1$ gets the sign of $-1$ wrong.
la-f-abs-k18 · level facile · notes
The absolute value of a real number is always $\ge 0$. Which $x \in \R$ satisfy $|x| \le -1$?
  1. $-1 \le x \le 1$
  2. $x \le -1$
  3. $x \ge 1$
  4. No $x$
Explanation. $|x| \ge 0 > -1$ for every $x$: the inequality has no solution. The rule $-\varepsilon \le x \le \varepsilon$ holds only for $\varepsilon \ge 0$.
la-f-abs-k19 · level facile · notes
Which $x \in \R$ satisfy $|2x + 4| \le 0$?
  1. Only $x = -2$
  2. No $x \in \R$
  3. Every $x \le -2$
  4. Every $x \ge -2$
Explanation. An absolute value is $\ge 0$, so $|2x + 4| \le 0$ is equivalent to $2x + 4 = 0$, i.e. $x = -2$.
la-f-abs-k20 · level facile · notes
The absolute value is $|a| = a$ if $a \ge 0$ and $|a| = -a$ if $a < 0$. What is $|\pi - 4|$?
  1. $\pi + 4$
  2. $-\pi - 4$
  3. $4 - \pi$
  4. $\pi - 4$
Explanation. $\pi \approx 3.14 < 4$, so $\pi - 4 < 0$ and $|\pi - 4| = -(\pi - 4) = 4 - \pi$. $\pi - 4$ is negative and cannot be an absolute value.
la-f-abs-k21 · level facile · notes
The absolute value is $|a| = a$ if $a \ge 0$ and $|a| = -a$ if $a < 0$. For $x < 2$, what does $|x - 2| + x$ equal?
  1. $2 - 2x$
  2. $2$
  3. $2x - 2$
  4. $-2$
Explanation. For $x < 2$ we have $x - 2 < 0$, so $|x - 2| = 2 - x$ and the sum is $2 - x + x = 2$. $2x - 2$ uses $|x - 2| = x - 2$, valid only for $x \ge 2$.
la-f-abs-k22 · level facile · notes
What are the real solutions of the equation $|x - 5| = 3$?
  1. $x = -8$ and $x = -2$
  2. $x = 2$ and $x = 8$
  3. Only $x = 8$
  4. $x = -2$ and $x = 8$
Explanation. $x - 5 = 3$ or $x - 5 = -3$, i.e. $x = 8$ or $x = 2$: the points at distance $3$ from $5$. «Only $8$» forgets the negative case.
la-f-abs-k23 · level facile · notes
$|x - a|$ is the distance between the reals $x$ and $a$. For which $x \in \R$ does $|x - 1| = |x + 3|$ hold?
  1. $x = 2$
  2. $x = -1$
  3. $x = 1$
  4. $x = -3$
Explanation. $x$ is equidistant from $1$ and $-3$: it is the midpoint $\frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = -1$. Check: $|-2| = |2| = 2$.
la-f-abs-k24 · level facile · notes
For any two reals $x$ and $y$, what is $|x - y| - |y - x|$?
  1. $0$
  2. $2\,|x - y|$
  3. $2\,(x - y)$
  4. $-2\,|x - y|$
Explanation. $y - x = -(x - y)$ and $|-b| = |b|$ for every real $b$, so the two absolute values coincide and the difference is $0$.
la-f-abs-k25 · level facile · notes
By the triangle inequality with $k$ terms, $\big|\sum_{i=1}^{k} b_i\big| \le \sum_{i=1}^{k} |b_i|$. If $|b_i| \le 2$ for $i = 1, \dots, 5$, what is the largest possible value of $\big|\sum_{i=1}^{5} b_i\big|$?
  1. $32$
  2. $5$
  3. $10$
  4. $2$
Explanation. $\big|\sum_i b_i\big| \le \sum_i |b_i| \le 5 \cdot 2 = 10$, and the value $10$ is reached with $b_i = 2$ for every $i$. $32 = 2^5$ multiplies instead of adding.
la-f-abs-k26 · level facile · notes
By the triangle inequality, $|d - e| \le |d - f| + |e - f|$ (for all $d, e, f \in \R$). Knowing that $|d - f| \le 3$ and $|e - f| \le 4$, what can be concluded about $|d - e|$?
  1. $|d - e| \ge 7$
  2. $|d - e| = 7$
  3. $|d - e| \le 7$
  4. $|d - e| \le 1$
Explanation. $|d - e| \le |d - f| + |e - f| \le 3 + 4 = 7$: the distance between $d$ and $e$ is at most the sum of the distances from $f$. One cannot say it equals $7$ or more.
la-f-abs-k27 · level facile · notes
In the triangle inequality $|b + c| \le |b| + |c|$ (for all $b, c \in \R$) we set $b = g - h$ and $c = h$. Which inequality is obtained?
  1. $|g| \le |g - h| + |h|$
  2. $|g - h| \le |g| - |h|$
  3. $|g| + |h| \le |g - h|$
  4. $|g + h| \le |g| - |h|$
Explanation. $b + c = g - h + h = g$, so $|g| \le |g - h| + |h|$, i.e. $|g| - |h| \le |g - h|$. The others reverse the direction or the signs.
la-f-abs-k28 · level facile · notes
By the reverse triangle inequality, $\big||g| - |h|\big| \le |g - h|$ for all $g, h \in \R$. Knowing that $|g - h| \le 1$ and $|h| = 5$, which interval surely contains $|g|$?
  1. $4 \le |g| \le 6$
  2. $|g| \le 1$
  3. $5 \le |g| \le 6$
  4. $0 \le |g| \le 5$
Explanation. $\big||g| - 5\big| \le |g - h| \le 1$ gives $-1 \le |g| - 5 \le 1$, i.e. $4 \le |g| \le 6$. For instance $g = 4$, $h = 5$ gives $|g| = 4$.
la-f-abs-k29 · level facile · notes
The absolute value is $|a| = a$ if $a \ge 0$ and $|a| = -a$ if $a < 0$. Which expression equals $|x|$ for every $x \in \R$?
  1. $\max\{x, -x\}$
  2. $\min\{x, -x\}$
  3. $\max\{x, 0\}$
  4. $-x$
Explanation. Of $x$ and $-x$ one is $\ge 0$: the larger of the two is $|x|$. $\max\{x, 0\}$ equals $0$ for $x < 0$, instead of $-x$.
la-f-abs-k30 · level facile · notes
How many integers $x$ satisfy $|x| < 3$?
  1. $7$
  2. $5$
  3. $6$
  4. $3$
Explanation. $|x| < 3$ is equivalent to $-3 < x < 3$: the integers are $-2, -1, 0, 1, 2$, that is $5$. $7$ also includes $\pm 3$ (non-strict inequality).
la-f-abs-k31 · level facile · notes
$|x - a|$ is the distance between the reals $x$ and $a$. Which inequality describes the reals $x$ at distance less than $2$ from the number $-1$?
  1. $|x| + 1 < 2$
  2. $|x + 1| < 2$
  3. $|x - 1| < 2$
  4. $|x + 2| < 1$
Explanation. The distance from $-1$ is $|x - (-1)| = |x + 1|$. $|x - 1|$ measures the distance from $+1$; $|x + 2| < 1$ swaps centre and radius.
la-f-abs-k32 · level ipotesi · notes
The rule $|a| < \varepsilon \Leftrightarrow -\varepsilon < a < \varepsilon$ holds for $\varepsilon > 0$. A student solves $|x - 3| < -2$ by writing $2 < x - 3 < -2$. What is wrong?
  1. The rule requires $\varepsilon > 0$
  2. The directions had to be reversed
  3. Both sides had to be squared
  4. Nothing: one gets $5 < x < 1$
Explanation. With $\varepsilon = -2 < 0$ the rule does not apply: $|x - 3| \ge 0 > -2$, so the inequality has no solution.
la-f-abs-k33 · level ipotesi · notes
To derive $|d - e| \le |d - f| + |e - f|$ from the triangle inequality $|b + c| \le |b| + |c|$, which substitution is used (with which fact)?
  1. $b = d - e$, $c = f$, and $|b\,c| = |b|\,|c|$
  2. $b = d + f$, $c = e + f$, and nothing else
  3. $b = d - f$, $c = f - e$, and $|f - e| = |e - f|$
  4. $b = d$, $c = -e$, and $|{-e}| = e$
Explanation. With $b = d - f$ and $c = f - e$ we get $b + c = d - e$, so $|d - e| \le |d - f| + |f - e|$, and finally $|f - e| = |e - f|$.

A.2. Modular arithmetic (46)

la-f-mod-01 · level facile · notes
In the Euclidean division of $n = -7$ by $m = 3$ (with $n = q\,m + r$, $0 \le r < |m|$), what are $q$ and $r$?
  1. $q = -3$, $r = 2$
  2. $q = -2$, $r = -1$
  3. $q = 2$, $r = -1$
  4. $q = -2$, $r = 1$
Explanation. $-7 = (-3)\cdot 3 + 2$ with $0 \le 2 < 3$. With $q = -2$ the remainder would be $-1$, negative: not the standard convention.
la-f-mod-02 · level facile · notes
$n \equiv k \pmod{m}$ means that $n$ and $k$ have the same remainder in Euclid's division by $m$. Which congruence is true?
  1. $23 \equiv 13 \pmod{6}$
  2. $23 \equiv 13 \pmod{5}$
  3. $23 \equiv 13 \pmod{3}$
  4. $23 \equiv 13 \pmod{4}$
Explanation. $23 - 13 = 10$, divisible by $5$ but not by $3$, $4$ or $6$; indeed $23$ and $13$ both have remainder $3$ in the division by $5$.
la-f-mod-03 · level teorema · notes
Which statement of Euclid's division is correct (for integers $n, m$ with $m \neq 0$)?
  1. there exist unique integers $q, r$ with $n = q\,m + r$ and $0 \le r < |m|$
  2. there exist unique integers $q, r$ with $n = q\,m + r$ and $0 \le r \le |m|$
  3. there exist unique integers $q, r$ with $n = q\,m + r$ and $|r| < |m|$
  4. there exist integers $q, r$ with $n = q\,m + r$ and $0 \le r < |m|$, but they are not unique
Explanation. With $0 \le r \le |m|$ or $|r| < |m|$ uniqueness is lost: for instance $6 = 2\cdot 3 + 0 = 1\cdot 3 + 3$ and $7 = 2\cdot 3 + 1 = 3\cdot 3 - 2$.
la-f-mod-04 · level facile · notes
When does $n \equiv k \pmod{m}$ hold?
  1. when $n$ mod $k = m$
  2. when $m$ divides $n\,k$
  3. when $m$ divides $n - k$
  4. when $n - k$ divides $m$
Explanation. $n$ and $k$ are congruent modulo $m$ if they have the same remainder in the division by $m$, i.e. if $n - k = q\,m$ for some integer $q$.
la-f-mod-05 · level ipotesi · notes
Euclid's division: for integers $n$, $m \neq 0$ there exist integers $q, r$ with $n = q\,m + r$ and $0 \le r < |m|$. In the proof of existence, which result guarantees that the remainder $r$ exists?
  1. the uniqueness of $q$ and $r$, proved before existence
  2. well-ordering: the nonempty set $S = \{n - km \ge 0 : k \in \Z\}$ has a smallest element
  3. the pigeonhole principle applied to the possible remainders $0, 1, \dots, |m| - 1$
  4. the triangle inequality $|n - km| \le |n| + |k|\,|m|$ for every $k \in \Z$
Explanation. $r$ is the minimum of $S$; if $r \ge |m|$, also $r - |m|$ would be in $S$, contradicting minimality: hence $0 \le r < |m|$. Uniqueness is proved separately and does not give existence.
la-f-mod-06 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove uniqueness in Euclid's division one assumes $n = q_1 m + r_1 = q_2 m + r_2$ with $0 \le r_1, r_2 < |m|$ ($m \neq 0$). Which argument concludes the proof?
  1. $|r_2 - r_1| \le |r_1| + |r_2| < 2|m|$, by the triangle inequality
  2. $m$ is prime, hence it divides $q_1 - q_2$ or $r_2 - r_1$
  3. $r_2 - r_1 = (q_1 - q_2)\,m$ is a multiple of $m$ and $|r_2 - r_1| < |m|$: it is $0$
  4. $r_1$ and $r_2$ are both the minimum of the same set, by well-ordering
